Non-relativistic regime and topology: topological term in the Einstein equation
Quentin Vigneron

Abstract
We study the non-relativistic (NR) limit of relativistic spacetimes in relation with the topology of the Universe. We first show that the NR limit of the Einstein equation is only possible in Euclidean topologies, i.e. for which the covering space is 𝔼 3 \mathbb{E}^{3} . We interpret this result as an inconsistency of general relativity in non-Euclidean topologies and propose a modification of that theory which allows for the limit to be performed in any topology. For this, a second reference non-dynamical connection is introduced in addition to the physical spacetime connection. The choice of reference connection is related to the covering space of the spacetime topology. Instead of featuring only the physical spacetime Ricci tensor, the modified Einstein equation features the difference between the physical and the reference Ricci tensors. This theory should be considered instead of general relativity if one wants to study a universe with a non-Euclidean topology and admitting a non-relativistic limit.



1]Institute of Astronomy, Faculty of Physics, Astronomy and Informatics, Nicolaus Copernicus University, Grudziadzka 5, Toruń, 87-100, Poland

1
Introduction and summary

The goal of this paper is to study the link between the non-relativistic (NR) regime, the relativistic regime and the topology of the Universe. In particular, a new modification of the Einstein equation in relation with the topology will be proposed. The logic of this study can be summarised into three main questions which are presented below, along with the main results of the paper. To give a visual understanding if these results, we have also condensed them into a sequence of figures at the end of this introduction (Figure
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
, and
5
). Each new implementation between one figure to the next one is highlighted in red.

Non-relativistic theory and topology
: In cosmology, we usually use two types of theories: Lorentz invariant theories, i.e. relativistic theories, such as general relativity in the current paradigm; and a Galilean invariant theory, i.e. a non-relativistic theory, which is Newton’s theory. While general relativity is well defined for any topology we can physically consider for the Universe, Newton’s theory can only be defined on a spatial Euclidean topology, i.e. when the covering space
\tilde{\Sigma}
is the Euclidean space
\mathbb{E}^{3}
(see section
2.1
for more details on the definition of Euclidean and non-Euclidean topologies). In other words, while we have a relativistic theory defined for any topology, the non-relativistic theory we have is only defined for Euclidean topologies. This leads to a first question (Figure
1
):

(1)

What equations, i.e. theory, should we consider to describe the non-relativistic regime of gravitation in non-Euclidean topologies?

This question was physically motivated in
[
35
]
and properly formalised in
[
15
,
34
,
35
]
using a mathematical object called
Galilean structures
(see section
3.2
), which enables us to write Galilean invariant equations in the same language as general relativity, i.e. differential geometry. Using this object, we can formulate Newton’s equations as 4-dimensional equations on a 4-manifold: these are the so-called Newton-Cartan equations
[
14
, e.g.]
.

The answer to question (1) was given by
Künzle 1976
and fully studied in
Vigneron 2022b
,
Vigneron &
Roukema 2023
(see also the heuristic approach of
[
28
,
2
]
): the non-relativistic theory in non-Euclidean topologies is given by a modified Newton-Cartan equation (Figure
2
). This theory could be called “non-relativistic-non-Euclidean theory”, and in this sense Newton’s theory would be called the “non-relativistic-Euclidean theory”. In
[
36
]
, we used the term
non-Euclidean Newtonian theory
(hereafter NEN theory) which we keep for the remaining of this paper. This theory has to be understood as
an extension
(to include non-Euclidean topologies, e.g. spherical) and
not a modification
of Newtonian gravitation, since Newton’s second law is still valid and Galilean invariance is kept, contrary to MOND theory for instance. In other words, Newton’s theory and the NEN theory are essentially the same NR theory of gravitation, but defined on a different topology. We showed in
[
36
]
, that this theory is the only physical Galilean invariant theory which can be defined on non-Euclidean topologies (spherical or hyperbolic in the case of
[
36
]
) and is second order. The theory is presented in Appendix
A
. It has the same equations as Newton’s theory, but with the presence of a non-zero spatial curvature in the spatial derivatives.

Non-relativistic limit and the Einstein equation
: While we generally do not consider the non-relativistic regime to be a fundamental feature of the Universe, it is still used to study gravitation at the classical and quantum levels
[
13
,
11
, e.g.]
. For this reason, it is generally assumed that relativistic gravitation should include in a certain limit NR gravitation. Our current understanding of the link between these two regimes is that Einstein’s equation is compatible with NR gravitation in a Euclidean topology, i.e. compatible with Newton’s theory. We therefore raise a second question (Figure
1
):

(2)

Is the Einstein equation compatible with the non-relativistic regime for any topology?

This question is somehow equivalent as asking if Einstein’s equation is compatible with the NEN theory. Checking this compatibility is the first goal of this paper.

After properly defining the non-relativistic limit in Section
3
, we will show in Section
4
that the answer to that question is “No”: the NR limit of the Einstein equation only exists if the topology is Euclidean (Figure
3
).

Topological term in the Einstein equation
: We argue in Section
4.3
that this result can be interpreted as an inconsistency of general relativity for non-Euclidean spatial topologies, and that full mathematical compatibility between the relativistic and the NR regimes of gravitation for any topology should be required. Consequently, Einstein’s equation needs to be modified, which leads to a third question (Figure
4
):

(3)

What relativistic equation admitting a non-relativistic limit in any topology should we consider?

The second goal of this paper is to propose what is to our opinion the right relativistic equation. It is given by a slight variation of the bi-connection theory of
Rosen 1980
, where a term related to topology is added to the Einstein equation (Figure
5
).
This term depends on a reference curvature
\bar{R}_{\mu\nu}
defined from a reference connection
\bar{\nabla}
(and not necessarily a reference metric) that is chosen as function of the spacetime topology. Therefore, this theory is a bi-connection theory (the usual physical Lorentzian metric of general relativity is still present), which only differs from general relativity in non-Euclidean topologies, for which
\bar{R}_{\mu\nu}\not=0
, and should be considered instead of general relativity if one wants to study a universe with a non-Euclidean topology and admitting a NR limit.

We describe in more details that theory in Section
5
, and show in Section
6
that its NR limit is well defined for non-Euclidean topologies, the resulting theory in the limit being, as expected, the NEN theory derived in
[
36
]
. As a consistency check, we also derive the non-relativistic dictionary of the bi-connection theory in Section
6.3
. We conclude in Section
7
.

Figure 1:
In this scheme, the second and third columns represent a Universe with, respectively, a Euclidean spatial topology and a non-Euclidean one. The second and third lines represent the relativistic and non-relativistic regimes. The former is defined from local Lorentzian invariance, and is mathematically described by Lorentzian structures solution of the Einstein equation, which is defined in any topology. The latter is defined from local Galilean invariance and is mathematically described via Galilean structures (Section
3.2
) solution of the Newton-Cartan equation (i.e. Newton’s theory),
only
in the Euclidean case. “(1) What non-relativistic equation should we consider in non-Euclidean topologies?”, and “(2) Is the non-relativistic limit of the Einstein’s equation possible in non-Euclidean topologies?” were the two questions that initiated the work of this paper.

Figure 2:
The answer to the first question was found in
[
15
,
36
]
: the non-relativistic theory on non-Euclidean topologies is given by a modification of the Newton-Cartan equation, in which a spatial curvature term is added.

Figure 3:
Two new results appear on this third scheme: i) the NR limit fundamentally corresponds to a limit of structures, from a Lorentzian structure to a Galilean structure (Sections
3.3
and
3.4
), i.e. it is a limit between Lorentzian and Galilean invariance; ii) the NR limit of the Einstein equation is only possible in 4-manifolds with a Euclidean spatial topology (Section
4.2
).

Figure 4:
If we require the NR limit to exist in any topologies, then the relativistic equation to consider should feature an additional term with respect to the Einstein equation. “(3) What is that term?” is the third question we are interested in in this paper. Several arguments for why this question is fundamental are drawn in Section
4.3
.

Figure 5:
By adding in the Einstein equation a non-dynamical, reference, spacetime curvature
\bar{R}_{\mu\nu}^{\mathbb{R}\times\tilde{\Sigma}}
related to the covering space
{\mathbb{R}\times\tilde{\Sigma}}
of the spacetime topology, we allow for the NR limit to be performed in any topologies. In the follow up paper of this study
[
37
]
, we show that this new relativistic equation implies that the expansion becomes blind to the spatial curvature (i.e.
\Omega_{\not=K}=1,\ \forall\Omega_{K}
).

2
Definitions

2.1
What we mean by Euclidean and non-Euclidean topologies

In this paper, we will always consider 4-manifolds
\mathcal{M}
of the form
\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma
, with
\Sigma
a
closed
3-manifold. When talking about (non)-Euclidean topology, this will refer to the topology of
\Sigma
.

The classification of 3-dimensional topological spaces (Thurston’s classification
[
30
,
22
]
) says that any topology can be decomposed into a connected sum over eight irreducible classes of topology. Each class, or ‘prime topology’, is characterised by its covering space
\tilde{\Sigma}
. A topology within a class is characterised by a discrete isometry group
\Gamma
, such that
\Sigma=\tilde{\Sigma}/\Gamma
. In particular, a topology is said to be
Euclidean
if its covering space
\tilde{\Sigma}
is the Euclidean space
\mathbb{E}^{3}
, and it is said to be
non-Euclidean
if the covering space is different from
\mathbb{E}^{3}
(example: spherical if
\tilde{\Sigma}=\mathbb{S}^{3}
, hyperbolic if
\tilde{\Sigma}=\mathbb{H}^{3}
). More precision on these notions are given
Lachieze-Rey &
Luminet 1995
.

An important property which is of particular interest for this paper is:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}=\boldsymbol{0}\ \Rightarrow\ \textrm{Euclidean topology},

(1)

or equivalently,

\displaystyle\textrm{non-Euclidean topology}\ \Rightarrow\ \boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\not=\boldsymbol{0},

where
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}
is the Ricci curvature tensor of any Riemannian metric defined on
\Sigma
. In other words, Euclidean does not imply that the Ricci curvature tensor must be zero, but can be zero. It is not a geometrical notion but a topological notion, which is slightly more general as shown by (
1
).

We stress that this notion of Euclidean, spherical and hyperbolic where curvature is not necessarily constant is in fact, implicitly, the notion used in Cosmology. Indeed, the constant curvature
K
is only valid for the background spatial metric. The real curvature, even for
K=0
, i.e. the ‘Euclidean’ or ‘flat’ case, is non zero due to the presence of inhomogeneities. Furthermore, considering closed spatial sections is not a restriction with respect to cosmology. Indeed, as will be presented in Section
3.5
, because spatial boundary conditions are needed to solve either Einstein’s or Newton’s equations, a closed spatial manifold is (implicitly) always assumed in cosmology. Therefore for this reason, the type of manifolds and topologies considered in the Standard Model of Cosmology are exactly the ones described by the Thurston classification and used in the present paper. In particular, the Standard Model describes either a Euclidean, spherical or hyperbolic topology in the same sense given above (see
[
8
, e.g.]
for cosmological models not considering prime topologies).

We also stress that the term “non-Euclidean” in the name “non-Euclidean Newtonian theory” refers to topology in the same sense. This reflects the fact that the purpose of this theory is not to take into account curvature effects which could come from relativistic corrections, but effects of the topology on the non-relativistic regime.

2.2
Notations

In this paper, we will use three different spacetime structures on the same manifold: a Lorentzian structure
(\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{\nabla})
, a reference connection
\boldsymbol{\bar{\nabla}}
, and a Galilean structure
(\hat{\boldsymbol{h}},\boldsymbol{\tau},\hat{\boldsymbol{\nabla}})
. Because similar quantities will be defined in each case, we will use the following notations:

•

The quantities related to the Lorentzian structure will not have specific notation, except in Section
6
for the NR limit.

•

The quantities related to the reference structure will be denoted with a bar. We have
\bar{\nabla}_{\alpha}
,
\bar{\Gamma}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}
,
\bar{R}_{\alpha\beta}
,
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{\alpha\beta}
,
\bar{h}_{ij}
,
\bar{D}_{i}
.

•

The quantities related to the Galilean structure will be denoted with a hat. We have
\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}
,
\hat{\Gamma}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}
,
\hat{R}_{\alpha\beta}
,
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\alpha\beta}
,
\hat{h}^{\alpha\beta}
,
\hat{D}_{i}
.

We denote
\kappa\coloneqq 8\pi G
where
G
is the gravitational constant.

Throughout this paper, we denote indices running from 0 to 3 by Greek letters and indices running from 1 to 3 by Roman letters.

3
The Non-relativistic limit of Lorentzian spacetimes

We present in this section the NR limit used in this paper. As this limit is defined from a Lorentzian structure which is not necessarily solution of the Einstein equation, for now we will not apply the limit to this equation. This will be done in section
4
.

3.1
What limit?

Two limits are usually considered in general relativity. First, the Post-Minkowskian limit, where the Lorentzian metric
\boldsymbol{g}
is a perturbation of the Minkowski metric
\boldsymbol{\eta}
as
g_{\alpha\beta}=\eta_{\alpha\beta}+f_{\alpha\beta}
where
|f_{\alpha\beta}|\ll 1
and
|\partial_{\gamma}f_{\alpha\beta}|\ll 1
. The Einstein equation is then linearised at first order in
f_{\alpha\beta}
and its derivatives. This approach has a main drawback for the purpose of this paper: because the perturbation is defined with respect to the Minkowski metric, the spatial topology is necessarily Euclidean. One way to allow for a non-Euclidean topology would be to replace
\boldsymbol{\eta}
by a FLRW metric, which is the approach used in cosmology
[
1
]
. However, this would imply by definition the spatial expansion to be necessarily given by the Friedmann equations. This is not appropriate as the expansion law should not be an assumption but a result coming from the NR equations at the limit (as shown in
[
3
,
34
]
for Newton’s theory). Second, the standard Post-Newtonian limit, which also has drawbacks making it not suited for the purpose of this paper. In this limit, the components of the spacetime Lorentzian metric are expressed as a Taylor series of the speed of light for which each order depends on the Newtonian gravitational potential (e.g. chapter 39 in
[
21
]
). The main drawback is the assumption of a flat spatial background metric (with or without expansion) incompatible with non-Euclidean topologies.

Therefore, we need a general definition for what we call a NR limit regardless of the topology considered, along with a precise mathematical framework to perform this limit. In
[
36
]
, we argued that the best way of defining a NR theory regardless of the topology is to consider Galilean invariance as a fundamental principle and to use a mathematical object called
Galilean structures
(presented in section
3.2
). These objects allow for a formulation of Newton’s theory close to general relativity (this is the Newton-Cartan formulation
[
14
,
11
]
). In this formalism the physical equation relates the energy content of a (spacetime) 4-manifold to its Galilean (spacetime) curvature, similarly to general relativity with Lorentzian structures. This formulation of Newton’s theory also allows for a better conceptual description of expansion
[
35
]
. Therefore, by considering Lorentzian and Galilean invariances as the key properties characterising a NR limit, we give the following definition:

Definition 1

(Non-relativistic limit)
.

A non-relativistic limit, regardless of the topology, starts from a locally Lorentz invariant theory and tends in the limit
c\rightarrow\infty
to a locally Galilean invariant theory. The former theory is described by a Lorentzian structure and the latter by a Galilean structure.

Such a limit was first developed by
Künzle 1976
, and further studied by
[
4
,
24
,
31
,
11
, e.g.]
. It does not have the problems listed above for the usual limits used in general relativity: there are no assumptions on the curvature or topology of the spatial sections; there is no assumed background expansion; non-linearities for the fluid dynamics are present at the limit. For these reasons, this approach is usual considered to be the fundamental way of defining the NR limit.

The status of the 4-manifold on which is performed the limit is detailed in Section
3.3
after having introduced Galilean structures in Section
3.2
. The limit is explicitly given in Section
3.4
, with additional properties and discussions in Sections
3.6
,
3.7
and
3.8
.

3.2
Galilean structures

A
Galilean structure
defined on a differentiable 4-manifold
\mathcal{M}
is a set
(\boldsymbol{\tau},\hat{\boldsymbol{h}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\nabla}})
, where
\boldsymbol{\tau}
is an exact 1-form,
\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}
is a symmetric positive semi-definite (2,0)-tensor of rank 3, i.e. its signature is (0,+,+,+), with
\hat{h}^{\alpha\mu}\tau_{\mu}=0
, and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\nabla}}
is a connection compatible with
\boldsymbol{\tau}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}
, called a
Galilean connection
:

\hat{\nabla}_{\alpha}\tau_{\beta}=0\quad;\quad\hat{\nabla}_{\gamma}\hat{h}^{\alpha\beta}=0.

(2)

An important feature to have in mind regarding Galilean structures is the fact that because there is no spacetime metric in
(\boldsymbol{\tau},\hat{\boldsymbol{h}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\nabla}})
, i.e. rank 4 semi-positive symmetric tensor, then there is no duality between forms and vectors, and therefore no means of raising or lowering indices from
(\boldsymbol{\tau},\hat{\boldsymbol{h}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\nabla}})
alone.

In this framework, a vector
\boldsymbol{u}
is called a
\tau
-timelike vector
if
u^{\mu}\tau_{\mu}\not=0
or a
unit

\tau
-timelike vector if
u^{\mu}\tau_{\mu}=1
, and a (n,0)-tensor
\boldsymbol{T}
is called
spatial
if
\tau_{\mu}{T^{...\overset{\overset{\alpha}{\downarrow}}{\mu}...}}=0
for all
\alpha\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
. The exact 1-form
\boldsymbol{\tau}
defines a foliation
\{\Sigma_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
in
\mathcal{M}
, where
\Sigma_{t}
are spatial hypersurfaces in
\mathcal{M}
defined as the level surfaces of the scalar field
t
, with
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}t
. The tensor
\boldsymbol{h}
naturally defines a (spatial) Riemannian metric on
\{\Sigma_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
. Finaly, the coefficients
\hat{\Gamma}^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}
of the Galilean connection define a (spacetime) Riemann tensor
{\hat{R}^{\sigma}}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
on
\mathcal{M}
with the usual formula

{\hat{R}^{\sigma}}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\coloneqq 2\,\partial_{[\beta}\hat{\Gamma}^{\sigma}_{\gamma]\alpha}+2\,\hat{\Gamma}^{\sigma}_{\mu[\beta}\hat{\Gamma}^{\mu}_{\gamma]\alpha}.

(3)

Unlike for Lorentzian structures, from the knowledge of
\boldsymbol{\tau}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}
, the Galilean connection
\hat{\boldsymbol{\nabla}}
is not unique and is defined up to a unit timelike vector
\boldsymbol{B}
(or equivalently, up to a spatial vector) and a two form
\kappa_{\alpha\beta}
. Its coefficients
\hat{\Gamma}_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}
take the form

\hat{\Gamma}_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}=\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\Gamma}^{{\kern-9.53786pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 4.94446pt}}_{{\kern-19.39886pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.94446pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\Gamma}^{{\kern-6.37524pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 3.0625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\Gamma}^{{\kern-5.50024pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 2.1875pt}}_{{\kern-12.54382pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.1875pt}}}_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}+2\tau_{(\alpha}\kappa_{\beta)\mu}\hat{h}^{\mu\gamma},

(4)

where

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\Gamma}^{{\kern-9.53786pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 1.50208pt}}}_{\alpha\beta}\right)+B^{\gamma}\partial_{(\alpha}\tau_{\beta)},

(5)

and where
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\boldsymbol{b}^{{\kern-8.2392pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 3.6458pt}}_{{\kern-18.1002pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.6458pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\boldsymbol{b}^{{\kern-8.2392pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 3.6458pt}}_{{\kern-18.1002pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.6458pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\boldsymbol{b}^{{\kern-5.8648pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 2.55206pt}}_{{\kern-12.90839pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.55206pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\boldsymbol{b}^{{\kern-5.13564pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 1.82289pt}}_{{\kern-12.17921pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.82289pt}}}
is the projector orthonormal to the unit
\tau
-timelike vector
\boldsymbol{B}
, defined with

\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-8.11006pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 1.50208pt}}}_{\alpha\mu}B^{\mu}\coloneqq 0\quad;\quad\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-8.11006pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-17.97107pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-17.97107pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-5.41565pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 2.1029pt}}_{{\kern-12.45923pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.1029pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-4.81482pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 1.50208pt}}_{{\kern-11.8584pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.50208pt}}}_{\alpha\mu}\hat{h}^{\mu\beta}\coloneqq{\delta_{\alpha}}^{\beta}-\tau_{\alpha}B^{\beta}.

(6)

This type of structures is invariant under local Galilean transformations
[
14
]
.
\boldsymbol{\tau}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}
define a
preferred foliation
, i.e. a preferred space direction, in
\mathcal{M}
, hence corresponding to the Newtonian picture of spacetime.

Finally, the spatial Riemanian tensor
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}
induced by
\hat{h}^{\mu\nu}
on the
\tau
-foliation is given by (see Section 3. of
[
18
]
)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}={\hat{R}^{\alpha}}_{\gamma\sigma\upsilon}\hat{h}^{\beta\gamma}\hat{h}^{\sigma\mu}\hat{h}^{\upsilon\nu},

(7)

and the induced spatial Ricci tensor
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\alpha\beta}
is given by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\alpha\beta}={\hat{R}}_{\mu\nu}\hat{h}^{\alpha\mu}\hat{h}^{\beta\nu}.

(8)

3.3
Status of the 4-manifold within the limit

As presented in section
3.1
, the principle behind a NR limit is to start from a Lorentzian structure (
\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}},\accentset{\lambda}{\Gamma}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}
) and end-up with a Galilean structure
(\boldsymbol{\tau},\hat{\boldsymbol{h}},{\hat{\Gamma}}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta})
for
\lambda\coloneqq 1/c^{2}\rightarrow 0
, with
c
the speed of light. We explain in Section
3.6
why we parametrise as function of
\lambda\coloneqq 1/c^{2}
.

Because both structures must be defined on a manifold, then the most general way of defining a NR limit is to assume that the manifold on which the Lorentzian structure is defined is also parametrised by
\lambda
:

\accentset{\lambda}{\mathcal{M}}{\rm\ equipped\ with\ }(\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}},\accentset{\lambda}{\Gamma}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta})\quad\overset{\lambda\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}\quad\accentset{(0)}{\mathcal{M}}{\rm\ equipped\ with\ }(\boldsymbol{\tau},\hat{\boldsymbol{h}},{\hat{\Gamma}}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}).

(9)

We argue in what follows why we should actually consider
\accentset{\lambda}{\mathcal{M}}
to be the same for any
\lambda
.

An interpretation we can make of the mathematical objects used in general relativity is the following: the 4-manifold
\mathcal{M}
is the object describing our Universe and the Lorentzian structure
(\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{\nabla})
defined of
\mathcal{M}
is the object describing physical measurements in the Universe. The metric defines measurement of distances; the connection defines how we compare physical properties between two points in the Universe. That measurement is constrained by a physical equation: the Einstein equation. Then, a NR limit corresponds to approximating this ‘relativistic measurement’, for which, in particular, their exists an absolute velocity, by a ‘non-relativistic measurement’, for which there are a preferred space
\boldsymbol{\hat{h}}
and time
\boldsymbol{\tau}
directions with respect to which distances should be measured.

In this view, a NR limit should only be a change of definition of physical measurement, and not a change of Universe, i.e. the ‘manifold Universe’
\mathcal{M}
should not change under the procedure
c\rightarrow\infty
. Therefore, it seems more physical, or natural, to require a NR limit to be defined as follows:

\mathcal{M}{\rm\ equipped\ with\ }(\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}},\accentset{\lambda}{\Gamma}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta})\quad\overset{\lambda\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}\quad\mathcal{M}{\rm\ equipped\ with\ }(\boldsymbol{\tau},\hat{\boldsymbol{h}},{\hat{\Gamma}}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}).

(10)

It is only
a limit of structures
, i.e. the manifold on which is defined the Lorentzian and the Galilean structures does not change and is the same for both.
Therefore, the topology of
\mathcal{M}
, and especially the topology of
\Sigma
(since we consider
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma
), is unchanged when taking the NR limit.
This is a very important property that will be considered when applying the limit on the Einstein equation.

The approach (
9
) is similar to the ‘active point of view’ and the approach (
10
) to the ‘passive point of view’ used to describe perturbation theory in cosmology
[
20
]
. Therefore, we expect (
9
) and (
10
) to be equivalent and the above property (i.e. topology being fixed when performing the limit) to hold even in the first approach. In other words, approach (
9
) might require
\accentset{{\lambda_{1}}}{\mathcal{M}}
to be diffeomorphic to
\accentset{{\lambda_{2}}}{\mathcal{M}}
, which would imply their topology to be the same, and therefore, the topology to be still unchanged during the limiting procedure.

3.4
The limit

A NR limit satisfying property (
10
) was developed by
Künzle 1976
and generalised by
Rendall 1992
. The principle is to consider a 4-manifold
\mathcal{M}
and a 1-parameter family of Lorentzian structures
\{\overset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}},\overset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{\nabla}}\}_{\lambda>0}
on
\mathcal{M}
that depends smoothly on
\lambda
, such that

\displaystyle\accentset{\lambda}{g}^{\alpha\beta}=\hat{h}^{\alpha\beta}+\lambda\,\accentset{(1)}{g}^{\,\alpha\beta}+\lambda^{2}\,\accentset{(2)}{g}^{\,\alpha\beta}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3}),

(11)

\displaystyle\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{1}{\lambda}\tau_{\alpha}\tau_{\beta}+\accentset{(0)}{g}_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda\,\accentset{(1)}{g}_{\alpha\beta}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}),

(12)

where
\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}
is a positive semi-definite (2,0)-tensor of rank 3 on
\mathcal{M}
, and
\boldsymbol{\tau}
a 1-form. An important property is that the
n
-th order of the metric contravariant components are not the inverse of the
n
-th order of the covariant components, i.e.
\accentset{(n)}{g}^{\,\alpha\mu}\,\accentset{(n)}{g}_{\mu\beta}\not=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}
. Instead we have by definition
\accentset{\lambda}{g}^{\alpha\mu}\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\mu\beta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}
, which, with (
11
) and (
12
), leads to

\displaystyle-\tau_{\mu}\hat{h}^{\mu\alpha}=0,

(13)

\displaystyle-\accentset{(1)}{g}^{\,\alpha\mu}\,\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\beta}+\hat{h}^{\alpha\mu}\accentset{(0)}{g}_{\mu\beta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta},

(14)

\displaystyle-\accentset{(2)}{g}^{\,\alpha\mu}\,\tau_{\mu}\tau_{\beta}+\accentset{(1)}{g}^{\,\alpha\mu}\,\accentset{(0)}{g}_{\mu\beta}+\hat{h}^{\alpha\mu}\,\accentset{(1)}{g}_{\mu\beta}=0,

(15)

for the first three orders. Equation (
14
) leads to

\hat{h}^{\alpha\mu}\,\accentset{(0)}{g}_{\mu\beta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}-\tau_{\beta}B^{\alpha},

(16)

where we denote
B^{\alpha}\coloneqq-\tau_{\mu}\accentset{(1)}{g}^{\,\mu\alpha}
, with
B^{\mu}\tau_{\mu}=1
. Then we can write
\accentset{(1)}{g}^{\,\alpha\beta}
and
\accentset{(0)}{g}_{\alpha\beta}
as

\displaystyle\accentset{(1)}{g}^{\,\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-B^{\alpha}B^{\beta}+k^{\alpha\beta},

(17)

\displaystyle\accentset{(0)}{g}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-8.11006pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-17.97107pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-8.11006pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-17.97107pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-5.41565pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 2.1029pt}}_{{\kern-12.45923pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.1029pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-4.81482pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 1.50208pt}}_{{\kern-11.8584pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.50208pt}}}_{\alpha\beta}-2\phi\tau_{\alpha}\tau_{\beta},

(18)

where
k^{\mu\nu}
is spatial (i.e.
k^{\mu\alpha}\tau_{\mu}=0
),
\phi
is an arbitrary scalar, and
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-8.11006pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-17.97107pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-8.11006pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-17.97107pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-5.41565pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 2.1029pt}}_{{\kern-12.45923pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.1029pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-4.81482pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 1.50208pt}}_{{\kern-11.8584pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.50208pt}}}_{\alpha\beta}
is defined in equation (
6
) and corresponds to the projector orthogonal to
\boldsymbol{B}
with respect to the tensors
\boldsymbol{\tau}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}
. Then, computing the Levi-Civita connection of
\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}}
, we find

\displaystyle\accentset{\lambda}{\Gamma}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\hat{h}^{\gamma\mu}\left(\tau_{\alpha}\partial_{[\mu}\tau_{\beta]}+\tau_{\beta}\partial_{[\mu}\tau_{\alpha]}\right)+\left(2\phi\hat{h}^{\gamma\mu}+\accentset{(1)}{g}^{\,\gamma\mu}\right)\left(\tau_{\alpha}\partial_{[\mu}\tau_{\beta]}+\tau_{\beta}\partial_{[\mu}\tau_{\alpha]}\right)

(19)

\displaystyle\qquad+\hat{h}^{\gamma\mu}\left(\partial_{(\alpha}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-8.11006pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-17.97107pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-8.11006pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-17.97107pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-5.41565pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 2.1029pt}}_{{\kern-12.45923pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.1029pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-4.81482pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 1.50208pt}}_{{\kern-11.8584pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.50208pt}}}_{\beta)\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-8.11006pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-17.97107pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-8.11006pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-17.97107pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-5.41565pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 2.1029pt}}_{{\kern-12.45923pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.1029pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-4.81482pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 1.50208pt}}_{{\kern-11.8584pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.50208pt}}}_{\alpha\beta}\right)+B^{\gamma}\partial_{(\alpha}\tau_{\beta)}+\tau_{\alpha}\tau_{\beta}\hat{h}^{\gamma\mu}\partial_{\mu}\phi+\mathcal{O}(\lambda).

If the 1-form
\boldsymbol{\tau}
is exact, which we will suppose from now
1
1

1

It is possible to consider
\boldsymbol{\tau}
not exact, and even
\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{\tau}\not=0
, but this requires torsion which we do not consider in this paper (see e.g.
[
10
]
).
, the connection
\accentset{\lambda}{\Gamma}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}
has a regular limit for
\lambda\rightarrow 0
which corresponds to a Galilean connection
\hat{\Gamma}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}
compatible with
\boldsymbol{\tau}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}
:

\displaystyle\accentset{\lambda}{\Gamma}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\hat{\Gamma}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-9.53786pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 4.94446pt}}_{{\kern-19.39886pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.94446pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-9.53786pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 4.94446pt}}_{{\kern-19.39886pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.94446pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-6.37524pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 3.0625pt}}_{{\kern-13.41882pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.0625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-5.50024pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 2.1875pt}}_{{\kern-12.54382pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.1875pt}}}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}+\tau_{\alpha}\tau_{\beta}\hat{h}^{\gamma\mu}\partial_{\mu}\phi+\mathcal{O}(\lambda),

(20)

where
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-9.53786pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 4.94446pt}}_{{\kern-19.39886pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.94446pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-9.53786pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 4.94446pt}}_{{\kern-19.39886pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.94446pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-6.37524pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 3.0625pt}}_{{\kern-13.41882pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.0625pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{B}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Gamma^{{\kern-5.50024pt{\boldsymbol{B}}\kern 2.1875pt}}_{{\kern-12.54382pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40326pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.48228pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20163pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.1875pt}}}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}
is defined in equation (
5
). This Galilean connection is not the most general possible because the 2-form
\kappa_{\alpha\beta}
[defined in equation (
4
)] is necessarily exact as we have
\kappa_{\alpha\beta}=\tau_{[\alpha}\partial_{\beta]}\phi
.

In summary, from the Taylor series (
11
) and (
12
) with
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}t
, property (
10
) is fulfilled. To our knowledge, there is no proof that (
11
) and (
12
) is the only way to define a limit fulfilling that property. However, the leading orders of these Taylor series are motivated by the Minkowski metric, as shown in the next section. For this reason, we think it is unlikely that another form than (
11
) and (
12
) is possible. The same conclusion is drawn in
[
24
]
, where the author analyses the mathematical properties of this limit starting from a restricted set of axioms in the case of a flat spatial metric
\hat{h}^{\alpha\beta}
.
2
2

2

[
24
]
considers the more general case where odd powers of
1/c
are present in the Taylor series. However, they are only physical, i.e. cannot be set to zero with a gauge transformation, from the order
c^{-1}
in
g_{\mu\nu}
and the order
c^{-5/2}
for
g^{\mu\nu}
. Therefore, this does not change the main results of the present paper as it relies on the leading orders.

Remark 1
.

\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\mu\nu}
is singular for
\lambda\rightarrow 0
. It is possible to define a non-singular limit of the metric by changing the property
\accentset{\lambda}{g}^{\alpha\mu}\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\mu\beta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}
into
\accentset{\lambda}{g}^{\alpha\mu}\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\mu\beta}=\lambda\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}
as in
[
7
]
. The subsequent calculation remains however the same. So we will stick to the definition (
12
) which is most often used.

3.5
Local or global limit?

As we will see in Section
4.2
, this limit applied on the Einstein equation will imply a topological constraint. This will be possible only if we consider the limit to hold globally on
\mathcal{M}
. But, other than for pure investigative purposes, why one would want to consider this hypothesis? Indeed, instead of considering
\boldsymbol{\tau}
to be exact, implying the limit to be defined globally,
\boldsymbol{\tau}
could be assumed only closed, and therefore the limit would hold only locally. From this, no topological constraints could be drawn.

This argument, while in general correct, does not apply from the moment we are solving the equations (which is generally what we want to do in physics). The reason is that in general relativity as well as in Newton’s theory, elliptic equations need to be solved: the Gauss-Codazzi equations for the former theory and the Poisson equation for the latter. These equations being elliptic, they require boundary conditions which can be of two forms: either an infinite spatial manifold with suitable integrability (in other words an isolated system), or a compact manifold (closed for a physically relevant situation).
3
3

3

The only case when boundary conditions are not necessary is when exact homogeneity is assumed. This is however of little physical relevance, even for cosmology, as inhomogeneities (at least as perturbations) are always needed to make physical measurements and predictions. Therefore, boundary conditions are required.
This need for boundary conditions is present everywhere in general relativity (see
[
6
]
for a discussion on this topic): e.g.

•

the Scalar-Vector-Tensor decompositions of symmetric 3-dimensional tensors used in standard perturbations is uniquely defined only with the above conditions
[
39
]
,

•

the use of the Fourier transform, or more generally harmonic decomposition, to study the linear regime in cosmology requires the same conditions (in fact for these reasons, the
\Lambda
CDM model is mathematically a model of a closed Universe),

•

most definitions of mass require asymptotical flatness,

as well as in Newton’s theory: e.g.

•

boundary conditions are required to obtain the Poisson equation (and therefore Newton’s law) from the Newtonian limit of general relativity
[
4
,
34
, e.g.]
.

•

expansion, usually introduced with a Hubble flow
[
12
,
23
, e.g.]
, is in fact fundamentally a non-local field appearing when compact boundary conditions are considered (instead of an isolated system) in the Newton-Cartan equation
[
34
]
.

These boundary conditions require the limit to hold everywhere on the domain they define. The boundary conditions considered in the present paper are the one of a closed manifold (the only physically relevant condition in cosmology, i.e. fulfilling the Copernican principle). Therefore, the limit should be defined everywhere on the manifold.
These arguments also hold at the level of the theory (general relativity or Newton’s theory): strictly speaking we never, and we cannot, use these theories only locally, as any resolution (in general) of their constitutive equations require boundary conditions.

In conclusion, from the moment we are solving the equations, we need to consider general relativity, its NR limit and the NR theory at the limit to hold globally.

Remark 2
.

‘Holding globally’ does not mean that the theory obtained in the limit, e.g. Newton’s theory, should give accurate physical results at all scales. It is a mathematical statement and requirement regarding the Taylor series (
11
) and (
12
) defining the limit, and the solutions of the equations. We expect on very large scales (illustrated by small wave numbers in the matter power spectrum) and around compact objects, the leading order to be not accurate anymore and post-non-relativistic (i.e. post-Newtonian) orders should be taken into account. But, in any cases, these relativistic orders need the leading order to exist to be defined, i.e. need the full Taylor series to be mathematically well-defined.

3.6
Interpretation of
\lambda

The Minkowski metric, in coordinates where
x^{0}
has the dimension of a time, can be written as
\eta_{\alpha\beta}=\text{diag}(-c^{2},1,1,1)
, where
c
is the speed of light. Its inverse is
\eta^{\alpha\beta}=\text{diag}(-c^{-2},1,1,1)
. Then assuming
c\rightarrow\infty
, these two matrices become

\displaystyle\eta_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\overset{c\rightarrow\infty}{\sim}\text{diag}(-c^{2},0,0,0),

\displaystyle\eta^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\overset{c\rightarrow\infty}{\sim}\text{diag}(0,1,1,1).

These leading orders for
c\rightarrow\infty
have the same form as the leading orders of
\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}}
for
\lambda\rightarrow 0
: in coordinates adapted to the foliation given by
\boldsymbol{\tau}
(i.e.
\tau_{\alpha}=\delta^{0}_{\alpha}
and
h^{\alpha\beta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{a}\delta^{\beta}_{b}h^{ab}
), we have
\accentset{(0)}{g}^{\,\alpha\beta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{a}\delta^{\beta}_{b}h^{ab}
and
\accentset{(-1)}{g}_{\alpha\beta}=\text{diag}(-1/\lambda,0,0,0)
. This shows that the limit
\lambda\rightarrow 0
can be seen as a limit where
c\rightarrow\infty
with
\lambda=c^{-2}
.

Therefore, the speed of light related to a Lorentzian metric
\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}}
of the family
\{\overset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}}\}_{\lambda>0}
depends on
\lambda
and is given by

c_{\lambda}=\lambda^{-1/2}.

(21)

This means that the family
\{\overset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}}\}_{\lambda>0}
of Lorentzian metrics defines a family of light-cones at each point of
\mathcal{M}
. The light-cone related to a metric
\accentset{\lambda_{1}}{\boldsymbol{g}}
is more open than the one related to a metric
\accentset{\lambda_{2}}{\boldsymbol{g}}
if
\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}
. This is represented in Figure
6
.

If one wants to set the speed of light to be 1, which corresponds to choosing a coordinate system such that
x^{0}=ct
, this is only possible for one Lorentzian metric
\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}}
. For all the other metrics, the speed of light in this coordinate system will differ from
1
. This property is really important as it tells us that when we will consider equations (and their NR limit) which should feature the speed of light, we cannot take
c=1
, and are obliged to take
c=\lambda^{-1/2}
. This will be the case for the norm of timelike vectors (section
3.7
) and for the Einstein equation (section
4
).

Figure 6:
Scheme of three light-cones of the family
\{\overset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}}\}_{\lambda>0}
of Lorentzian metrics at a point
X
in
\mathcal{M}
. The smaller
\lambda
is, the more open the light-cone is:
\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<\lambda_{3}
. The slice
\Sigma_{\tau}(X)
corresponds to the only hypersurface member of the foliation defined by
\boldsymbol{\tau}
and passing through
X
. The limit of the family of light-cones is this hypersurface. The green vector is an example of a vector which is g-timelike for the Lorentzian metric
\overset{\lambda_{1}}{\boldsymbol{g}}
, but spacelike for
\overset{\lambda_{2}}{\boldsymbol{g}}
and
\overset{\lambda_{3}}{\boldsymbol{g}}
, hence showing that no vector can be timelike for every
\overset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}}
(see section
3.7
).

3.7
Limit of
g
-timelike vectors

Because the notion of timelike vectors is defined in both structures, but is not equivalent, to distinguish between the two, we will call g-timelike vectors the ones related to a Lorentzian structure, and we already call
\tau
-timelike vectors the ones related to the Galilean structure.

Let us consider a vector
\boldsymbol{u}
which is ‘‘unit’’ g-timelike
4
4

4

Because the speed of light related to
\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}}
cannot be set to 1 for all
\lambda
, then “unit” means that the norm of
\boldsymbol{u}
is
c_{\lambda}
.
for every member of the family
\{\overset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}}\}_{\lambda>0}
, i.e.
u^{\mu}u^{\nu}\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\mu\nu}=-1/\lambda
. If we assume that this vector does not depend on
\lambda
, then for a sufficiently small
\lambda
we have
(u^{\mu}\tau_{\mu})^{2}=1
. As
\boldsymbol{u}
is
any
unit g-timelike vector, this cannot be possible. So,
\boldsymbol{u}
needs to depend on
\lambda
. We write it
\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{u}}
(more precisely we define a family
\{\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{u}}\}_{\lambda>0}
of vectors). Because it is unit for all
\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}}
, then we have

\accentset{\lambda}{u}^{\alpha}=\accentset{(0)}{u}^{\,\alpha}+\lambda\accentset{(1)}{u}^{\,\alpha}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}),

(22)

with
\accentset{(0)}{u}^{\,\mu}\,\tau_{\mu}=1
, and
\accentset{(1)}{u}^{\,\mu}\,\tau_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}\accentset{(0)}{u}^{\,\mu}\,\accentset{(0)}{u}^{\,\nu}\,\accentset{(0)}{g}_{\mu\nu}
. In addition, we can compute the covariant components of
\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{u}}
, defined as
\accentset{\lambda}{u}_{\alpha}\coloneqq\accentset{\lambda}{u}^{\mu}\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\mu\alpha}
, which become

\accentset{\lambda}{u}_{\alpha}=-\frac{1}{\lambda}\tau_{\alpha}+\accentset{(0)}{u}_{\alpha}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda),

(23)

where
\accentset{(0)}{u}_{\alpha}=\accentset{(0)}{u}^{\,\mu}\,\accentset{(0)}{g}_{\mu\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}\accentset{(0)}{u}^{\,\mu}\,\accentset{(0)}{u}^{\,\nu}\,\accentset{(0)}{g}_{\mu\nu}\tau_{\alpha}
. Because
\accentset{(0)}{u}^{\,\mu}\,\tau_{\mu}=1
, this implies that
a unit g-timelike vector
\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{u}}
for all members of
\{\overset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}}\}_{\lambda>0}
corresponds to a unit
\tau
-timelike vector at the limit
\lambda\rightarrow 0
. However, the reverse is not possible: a
\tau
-timelike vector can never correspond to a g-timelike vector for all
\lambda
.

3.8
Limit of the Lorentzian Riemann tensor

For the purpose of taking the limit of the Einstein equation, we present in this section the limit of the Riemann tensor related to a Lorentzian metric.

The family
\{\overset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}}\}_{\lambda>0}
defines a family of Riemann tensors
\{{\accentset{\lambda}{R}^{\sigma}}_{\gamma\alpha\beta}\}_{\lambda>0}
on
\mathcal{M}
. Using the expression of
{\accentset{\lambda}{R}^{\sigma}}_{\gamma\alpha\beta}
in terms of the coefficients of the connection,

{\accentset{\lambda}{R}^{\sigma}}_{\gamma\alpha\beta}=2\partial_{[\alpha}\accentset{\lambda}{\Gamma}^{\sigma}_{\beta]\gamma}+2\accentset{\lambda}{\Gamma}^{\sigma}_{\mu[\alpha}\accentset{\lambda}{\Gamma}^{\mu}_{\beta]\gamma},

(24)

we easily see that the expansion of
{\accentset{\lambda}{R}^{\sigma}}_{\gamma\alpha\beta}
in series of
\lambda
takes the form

{\accentset{\lambda}{R}^{\sigma}}_{\gamma\alpha\beta}={\hat{R}^{\sigma}}_{\gamma\alpha\beta}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda),

where
{\hat{R}^{\sigma}}_{\gamma\alpha\beta}
is the Riemann tensor associated with the Galilean connection coefficients
\hat{\Gamma}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}
given by (
20
). Consequently, the leading order of the Ricci tensor coefficients
\overset{\lambda}{R}_{\alpha\beta}
corresponds to the Ricci tensor of the Galilean connection:

\accentset{\lambda}{R}_{\alpha\beta}={\hat{R}}_{\alpha\beta}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda).

Because Galilean structures do not feature non-degenerate metrics, raising and lowering indices is not possible. Therefore the Riemann and Ricci tensors of Galilean connections are only defined as, respectively, a (1,3)-tensor and a (0,2)-tensor. Consequently, identities (for instance Bianchi identities) where the coefficients
{\accentset{\lambda}{R}^{\sigma}}_{\gamma\alpha\beta}
are raised or lowered by
\overset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{g}}
have no equivalent in Galilean structures. However, the limit of these identities gives additional constraints on the (Galilean) Riemann tensor
{\hat{R}^{\sigma}}_{\gamma\alpha\beta}
. From the leading order of the interchanged symmetry
\mathchoice{\accentset{\lambda}{R}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}}}}{\accentset{\lambda}{R}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}}}}{\accentset{\lambda}{R}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}}}}{\accentset{\lambda}{R}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}}}}=\mathchoice{\accentset{\lambda}{R}^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}}{\accentset{\lambda}{R}^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}}{\accentset{\lambda}{R}^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}}{\accentset{\lambda}{R}^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}}
, we obtain

\hat{h}^{\mu\beta}\mathchoice{\hat{R}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}{\mu}{\sigma}}}}{\hat{R}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}{\mu}{\sigma}}}}{\hat{R}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}{\mu}{\sigma}}}}{\hat{R}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}{\mu}{\sigma}}}}-\hat{h}^{\mu\alpha}\mathchoice{\hat{R}^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}{\mu}{\gamma}}}}{\hat{R}^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}{\mu}{\gamma}}}}{\hat{R}^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}{\mu}{\gamma}}}}{\hat{R}^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\sigma}{\mu}{\gamma}}}}=0,

(25)

and from the contracted second Bianchi identity
\accentset{\lambda}{g}^{\mu\nu}\accentset{\lambda}{\nabla}_{\mu}\accentset{\lambda}{R}_{\nu\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}\accentset{\lambda}{\nabla}_{\alpha}\accentset{\lambda}{R}_{\mu\nu}\accentset{\lambda}{g}^{\mu\nu}=0
, we obtain

\hat{h}^{\mu\nu}\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}{\hat{R}}_{\nu\alpha}-\hat{\nabla}_{\alpha}\frac{\hat{R}_{\mu\nu}\hat{h}^{\mu\nu}}{2}=0,

(26)

at leading order. Both of these equations are geometrical constraints on the Galilean structures. In particular, equation (
25
), called the Trautman condition, is essential to have an irrotational gravitational field
[
7
,
34
, see e.g]
, as expected for a NR theory.

Apart for the leading orders of the Bianchi identities, no other constraints on the Galilean structure appears from the limit. In particular, the spatial Ricci curvature
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\alpha\beta}
related to
\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}
is totally free, and therefore also the spatial topology. Contraints on this tensor can only appear once we assume the Lorentzian structures to be solutions of the Einstein equation.

Additional formulas related to the NR limit are presented in Appendix
C
.

4
Non-relativistic limit of the Einstein equation

In this section, we give the answer to the question (2) raised in the introduction, namely:
Is the Einstein equation compatible with the non-relativistic regime for any topology?

4.1
Limit of the energy-momentum tensor

The energy-momentum tensor
\accentset{\lambda}{T}^{\alpha\beta}
of a general fluid of 4-velocity
\accentset{\lambda}{u}^{\alpha}
can be written as

\accentset{\lambda}{T}^{\alpha\beta}=\accentset{\lambda}{\epsilon}\,\accentset{\lambda}{u}^{\alpha}\accentset{\lambda}{u}^{\beta}+2\accentset{\lambda}{q}^{(\alpha}\accentset{\lambda}{u}^{\beta)}+\accentset{\lambda}{p}\,\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{u}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\accentset{\lambda}{b}^{{\kern-5.77449pt{u}\kern 2.85834pt{\alpha}{\beta}}}_{{\kern-11.94557pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.85834pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{u}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\accentset{\lambda}{b}^{{\kern-5.77449pt{u}\kern 2.85834pt{\alpha}{\beta}}}_{{\kern-11.94557pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.85834pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{u}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\accentset{\lambda}{b}^{{\kern-4.97305pt{u}\kern 2.85834pt{\alpha}{\beta}}}_{{\kern-9.38097pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.85834pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{u}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\accentset{\lambda}{b}^{{\kern-4.97305pt{u}\kern 2.85834pt{\alpha}{\beta}}}_{{\kern-9.38097pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.85834pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}+\accentset{\lambda}{\pi}^{\alpha\beta},

(27)

where
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{u}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\accentset{\lambda}{b}^{{\kern-5.77449pt{u}\kern 2.85834pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-11.94557pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.85834pt{\alpha}{\beta}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{u}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\accentset{\lambda}{b}^{{\kern-5.77449pt{u}\kern 2.85834pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-11.94557pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.85834pt{\alpha}{\beta}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{u}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\accentset{\lambda}{b}^{{\kern-4.97305pt{u}\kern
