2.85834pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-9.38097pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.85834pt{\alpha}{\beta}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{u}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\accentset{\lambda}{b}^{{\kern-4.97305pt{u}\kern 2.85834pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-9.38097pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.85834pt{\alpha}{\beta}}}}\coloneqq\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\alpha\beta}+\lambda\accentset{\lambda}{u}_{\alpha}\accentset{\lambda}{u}_{\beta}
is the projector orthogonal to
\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{u}}
. The expansion series of
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{u}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{b}}^{{\kern-5.77449pt{u}\kern 2.85834pt}}_{{\kern-11.94557pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.85834pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{u}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{b}}^{{\kern-5.77449pt{u}\kern 2.85834pt}}_{{\kern-11.94557pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.85834pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{u}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{b}}^{{\kern-4.97305pt{u}\kern 2.85834pt}}_{{\kern-9.38097pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.85834pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{u}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{b}}^{{\kern-4.97305pt{u}\kern 2.85834pt}}_{{\kern-9.38097pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.85834pt}}}
is detailed in
C.1
. As
\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{T}}
has the dimension of an energy and
\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{u}}
the dimension of a velocity, then
\accentset{\lambda}{\epsilon}
has the dimension of a mass density. Therefore in the case of a dust fluid,
\accentset{\lambda}{\epsilon}
is a zeroth order term given by the rest mass density of the fluid:
\accentset{\lambda}{\epsilon}=\rho
for all
\lambda
. For a more general
matter fluid
, where the fluid elements have internal energy,
\accentset{\lambda}{\epsilon}
will have higher order terms
[
15
]
. Using equations (
22
) and (
94
) on the conservation law
\accentset{\lambda}{\nabla}_{\mu}\accentset{\lambda}{T}^{\mu\alpha}=0
, we obtain the Newtonian mass conservation law, i.e.
\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}\left(\rho\accentset{0}{u}^{\mu}\right)=0
,
only if

[
15
, see]

\displaystyle\accentset{\lambda}{p}=\mathcal{O}(1)\quad;\quad\accentset{\lambda}{q}^{\alpha}=\mathcal{O}(\lambda)\quad;\quad\accentset{\lambda}{\pi}^{\alpha\beta}=\mathcal{O}(1).

(28)

That is, the pressure
\accentset{\lambda}{p}
, the heat flux
\accentset{\lambda}{q}^{\alpha}
and the anisotropic stress
\accentset{\lambda}{\pi}^{\alpha\beta}
are all regular for
\lambda\rightarrow 0
. Therefore, once we consider that there is no non-relativistic process for which mass is not conserved, i.e. once we require to have
\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}\left(\rho\accentset{0}{u}^{\mu}\right)=0
in a non-relativistic theory
5
5

5

See
Stichel 2016
for an example of
\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}\left(\rho\accentset{0}{u}^{\mu}\right)\not=0
.
, then the conditions (
28
) must hold. In that case,
\accentset{\lambda}{T}^{\alpha\beta}
has a regular limit, and we have

\displaystyle\accentset{\lambda}{T}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\left[\rho\,\tau_{\alpha}\tau_{\beta}\right]+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-1}),

(29)

\displaystyle\accentset{\lambda}{T}_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\accentset{\lambda}{T}^{\mu\nu}\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\mu\nu}\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\left[\frac{\rho}{2}\tau_{\alpha}\tau_{\beta}\right]+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-1}),

(30)

\displaystyle\accentset{\lambda}{T}^{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\accentset{\lambda}{T}^{\mu\nu}\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\mu\nu}\accentset{\lambda}{g}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\left[\frac{\rho}{2}\hat{h}^{\alpha\beta}\right]+\mathcal{O}(1).

(31)

This regularity of
\accentset{\lambda}{T}^{\alpha\beta}
for
\lambda\rightarrow 0
is generally assumed in papers using the NR limit in order to obtain the Newton-Cartan equation from Einstein’s equation
[
15
,
4
, e.g.]
. The present section aimed at giving some physical insights supporting this hypothesis, without requiring
a priori
the Newton-Cartan equation to hold.

4.2
Limit of the Einstein equation

The Einstein equation features two constants: the cosmological constant
\Lambda
and the gravitational constant
G
. The energy-momentum tensor
\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{T}}
having the dimension of an energy, it must appear as “
\kappa\lambda^{2}\accentset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{T}}\,
” in the equation. However, from that equation alone the cosmological constant does not have,
a priori
, a preferred dimension. For instance, if we consider
\Lambda
to have the dimension of a curvature, then Einstein’s equation will feature the term
\Lambda\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\alpha\beta}
. Instead, if we consider that
\Lambda
has the dimension of a time
-2
, there will be
\lambda\Lambda\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\alpha\beta}
.

The limit of the Einstein equation needs to be performed on the twice covariant version, i.e. as a (0,2)-tensor equation, featuring the Ricci tensor. Considering the (2,0) or the (1,1) versions, or the formulation featuring the Einstein tensor, give either no or redundant information at the limit. Therefore we have two possibilities:

\displaystyle\accentset{\lambda}{R}_{\alpha\beta}=\kappa\lambda^{2}\left(\accentset{\lambda}{T}_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\accentset{\lambda}{T}\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\alpha\beta}\right)+\Lambda\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\alpha\beta},

(32)

or

\displaystyle\qquad\accentset{\lambda}{R}_{\alpha\beta}=\kappa\lambda^{2}\left(\accentset{\lambda}{T}_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\accentset{\lambda}{T}\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\alpha\beta}\right)+\lambda\Lambda\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\alpha\beta}.

(33)

For the choice “
\Lambda\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\alpha\beta}
”, the leading order of (
32
) is
\frac{1}{\lambda}\Lambda\tau_{\alpha}\tau_{\beta}+\mathcal{O}(1)=0
, implying
\Lambda=0
. Therefore, only the choice “
\lambda\Lambda\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\alpha\beta}
” allows for a non-zero cosmological constant. The leading order of (
33
) is

\displaystyle{\hat{R}}_{\alpha\beta}=\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\rho-\Lambda\right)\tau_{\alpha}\tau_{\beta}.

(34)

This equation along with the geometrical constraint (
25
) and the conservation law
{\hat{\nabla}}_{\mu}\left(\rho\accentset{0}{u}^{\mu}\right)=0
corresponds to the Newton-Cartan system.

This system has been derived from the Einstein equation without any assumptions on the spatial topology or on the spatial Ricci curvature tensor related to
\hat{h}^{\mu\nu}
and given by
{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}^{\alpha\beta}=\hat{h}^{\mu\alpha}\hat{h}^{\nu\beta}\hat{R}_{\mu\nu}
. Still, equation (
34
) necessarily implies
{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}^{\alpha\beta}=0
, which can be seen by projecting (
34
) twice along
\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}
6
6

6

This result can also be derived directly from the 1+3-Einstein system of equations related to the fluid velocity
\overset{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{u}}

[
33
, see chapter 4 in]
.
. As presented in Section
3.5
, this result holds globally.

Therefore, this implies that on the
\tau
-foliation, for which we recall the hypersurfaces are
\Sigma
, it is possible to define a metric (here
\hat{h}^{ij}
) whose Ricci tensor is zero. From property (
1
) this means that the topology of each hypersurface member of this foliation is a Euclidean topology, i.e. that
\Sigma
has an Euclidean topology. We recall that, as explained in section
3.3
, the NR limit does not change the topology of
\mathcal{M}
. Therefore, we have the following result:

Theorem 1
.

A solution of the Einstein equation on a 4-manifold
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma
with
\Sigma
closed, and which has a non-relativistic limit everywhere, as defined in Section
3.4
, requires the topology of the 3-manifold
\Sigma
to be Euclidean. The limit is the cosmological Newtonian theory.

The only assumption on the energy-momentum tensor for this result to hold is that it describes a matter fluid whose mass is conserved at the limit. In any case, the result holds in vacuum. The result is schematised in Figure
3
.

The fact that the NR limit of the Einstein equation leads to the Newton-Cartan equation and imposes the Galilean structure to be spatially flat is not a new result of course. It was first derived in
[
15
]
, and later emphasised by
[
18
,
19
]
.
However, Ref.
[
15
]
imposes the limit of the energy-momentum tensor so that it leads to the Newton-Cartan equation, while we showed that this equation is always obtained (for a matter energy-momentum tensor or in vacuum), even with the presence of the cosmological constant. Refs.
[
18
,
19
]
also only focus on discussing the spatial curvature, while the (fundamental) link to topology and global properties is not made. The present paper aims at emphasising the importance of this link. In particular, the discussions made in Sections
3.5
and
4.3
lead to the interpretation that this zero spatial curvature result should be considered more as an inconsistency within GR, than as a natural (logical) result that we should accept.

4.3
Missing term in the Einstein equation

The previous result leads to the third question raised in the introduction:
What relativistic equation should we consider in a 4-manifold with a non-Euclidean spatial topology if we want compatibility with the NR regime?
In other words, what term
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha\beta}
should we add in Einstein’s equation, i.e. considering

{G}_{\alpha\beta}=\kappa\lambda^{2}{T}_{\alpha\beta}-\lambda\Lambda{g}_{\alpha\beta}+{{\mathcal{T}}}_{\alpha\beta},

(35)

such that the NR limit exists in any topology (Figure
4
). That question always stands on a purely mathematical point of view. In the next two sections, we motivate it with physical arguments, and propose a possible origin for the term
\mathcal{T}_{\mu\nu}
. An answer to the question is given in Section
5
, and represented in Figure
5
.

4.3.1
Motivations for the modification

When one constructs a new relativistic theory, or even historically when Einstein proposed his equation, it is generally required that this theory should be compatible with Newton’s theory. The reason given is that Newton’s law has been tested to be successful in (e.g.) the solar system. However, as shown in
[
38
]
, in a NR theory (Newton’s theory or the NEN theory for spherical or hyperbolic topologies), the gravitational potential
\Phi

close
to a mass point is always given by Newton’s law, i.e.
\Phi=-GM/r+\mathcal{O}\left(r\right)
, and correcting terms appear as function of the topology chosen (
\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\mathbb{T}^{3}
,
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, …), i.e. as function of the boundary conditions chosen. Those terms are always negligible on scales small compared to the finite size of the Universe (i.e. size of the boundary conditions), which are the scales on which the law has been tested. This means that from observations, we cannot distinguish between different non-relativistic theories. In other words, observations only tell us that gravitation is well described by a NR calculation on (e.g.) solar system scales, but the precise NR theory to consider depends on the type of topology of the Universe, which is not known. Therefore, requiring a relativistic theory to be only compatible with Newton’s theory is a restriction with respect to what we learn from observations.

This suggests that the right constraint on any relativistic theory should be to require the compatibility with the NR regime in general (Newton’s theory, spherical NEN theory, hyperbolic NEN theory, …), the right NR theory depending on the topology of the Universe.
If we consider this more general constraint, then, because Einstein’s equation only has a NR limit in Euclidean topologies, then this equation needs to be modified to allow the limit to be performed in other topologies. This leads to the question raised in the beginning of Section
4.3
.

Another way of saying it is the following: in the case our Universe is, for instance, spherical, the result of Section
4.2
shows that it is strictly mathematically incompatible to use both Einstein’s equation and a non-relativistic calculation. Saying that we consider the calculation only locally does not save the game, since global conditions are always necessary (see Section
3.5
). In other words, Einstein’s equation cannot describe the NR regime of a universe with a non-Euclidean topology, and this at any scales. Of course this is not a problem if the topology of our Universe is Euclidean. But if it is not, which is not forbidden by observations and even suggested
[
5
,
9
, e.g.]
, then, because our Universe is filled with (mostly) NR matter (we could say the Universe is in a NR regime), we should modify the Einstein equation such that it has a NR limit regardless of the topology.

4.3.2
A possible origin: topological lagrangian multiplier

As explained in Section
3.3
, the NR limit does not change the topology of the 4-manifold
\mathcal{M}
. If we consider Einstein’s equation as resulting from a variational principle, the same arises. The action
S
is defined on a 4-manifold
\mathcal{M}
and among all the equations possible on
\mathcal{M}
for the physical variable (i.e.
\boldsymbol{g}
), we consider that the physical one is the one which extremalises the action, and is obtained with
\delta S=0
. Therefore the variational principle is performed on the manifold
\mathcal{M}
which is
a priori
defined, i.e. the variation of
S
does not change
\mathcal{M}
. This means that, when varying the action, the topology should already be fixed, implying that we are forbidden to consider paths for
\boldsymbol{g}
(when varying the action) for which the Riemann tensor is incompatible with the topology.

In classical mechanics, when constraints are applied on a variational principle, an additional term with a Lagrange multiplier must be added in the action. The same might be needed for the topology in general relativity where we would have an action of the form:

\displaystyle S=\int\sqrt{g}\left(R_{\mu\nu}g^{\mu\nu}+\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Z}}\cdot\boldsymbol{C}+\lambda\kappa\mathcal{L}_{\rm matter}\right),

(36)

where
\boldsymbol{C}=\boldsymbol{0}
would be a tensorial equation constraining the topology of
\mathcal{M}
and especially the covering space
\tilde{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
in the case
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma
, and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Z}}
would be the tensorial Lagrange multiplier associated to this contraint. The idea of the present approach is to say that the additional term
\mathcal{T}_{\mu\nu}
in equation (
35
) would result from the variation of
\sqrt{g}\,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Z}}\cdot\boldsymbol{C}
, i.e. from the fixation of the topology in the variational principle.

While this idea might be interesting as it would give a justification for equation (
35
) outside of considering the NR regime and limit, it is not sure whether or not it can be successfully followed. The reason is that, to our knowledge, there does not exist a local tensorial equation constraining topology in three our four dimensions. Therefore, the solution we will propose in Section
5
to the question raised in Section
4.3
will not follow the Lagrange multiplier approach.

5
Topological term in the Einstein equation with a bi-connection theory

5.1
Constraints on the missing term

If we require the NR limit to exist in non-Euclidean topologies we need to consider a modified Einstein equation (
35
) where the additional term
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
allows for spatial curvature.
From the limit alone, few constraints can be drawn for this term. For instance, considering the Taylor series (
29
)–(
31
) for the energy-momentum tensor, along with the relations

\displaystyle\accentset{\lambda}{{G}}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\left[\frac{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}{2}\tau_{\alpha}\tau_{\beta}\right]+\mathcal{O}(1)\quad;\quad\accentset{\lambda}{{G}}^{\alpha\beta}=\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\alpha\beta}-\frac{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}{2}\hat{h}^{\alpha\beta}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda),

(37)

this implies
\accentset{(-1)}{{\cal T}}_{\alpha\beta}\propto\tau_{\alpha}\tau_{\beta}
and
\accentset{(0)}{{\cal T}}^{\alpha\beta}
to be spatial and non-zero.
Apart for these few conditions, no other constraint is imposed on
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
by the limit. Therefore, there is substantial freedom on the extrapolation at full order of this term. We found four natural conditions to consider that would constrain this freedom:

1.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}=0
for a Euclidean topology.

2.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
is related to the class of topology of
\mathcal{M}
, i.e. to the covering space of
\mathcal{M}
.

3.

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
depends on second (or less) derivatives of the spacetime metric.

4.

The NR limit of the modified Einstein equation should exist in any topology.

The reason for Constraint 1. is to retrieve the Einstein equation in the Euclidean case. With Constraint 2. we reject the possibility of having a coupling term with the fluid. The reason is that we want
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}\not=0
even in vacuum. We also require
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
to be related only to the class of topology (i.e. the covering space) and not the precise topology. The reason for this comes from the Euclidean case:
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}=0
for any Euclidean topology, so we should expect the same behaviour in other classes, with
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
being the same within a topological class. Constraint 3. is chosen such that we keep a second order theory. We see no reason why the order of a theory should depend on the topology chosen. A consequence is that
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
must depend on an additional field other than the metric
\boldsymbol{g}
(due to Lovelock’s theorem
[
17
]
). We can require this field to be related to the class of topology. Constraint 4. is the reason for the modification.

Because
\mathcal{T}_{\mu\nu}
is related to topology, we call it
the topological term
.

5.2
The bi-connection theory

We found one relativistic equation fulfilling the above natural constraints. It is given by a slightly different theory than the bi-connection theory proposed by Rosen
[
25
]
. This might not be the only possibility and another set of constraints could also be proposed. Still we think that the solution we present in this paper is the simplest we can obtain.

5.2.1
Equations of the theory

Rosen’s bi-connection theory in its 1980 version
[
25
]
is defined on a 4-manifold
\mathcal{M}
equipped with a physical Lorentzian metric
g_{\alpha\beta}
(and its inverse
g^{\alpha\beta}
) with its Levi-Civita connection
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
, and a non-dynamical reference connection
\boldsymbol{\bar{\nabla}}
. This second connection is non-dynamical in the sense that it is the same for any physical metric and energy-momentum tensor. The action is

\displaystyle S=\int\sqrt{-g}\left[\left(R_{\mu\nu}-\bar{R}_{\mu\nu}\right)g^{\mu\nu}+\lambda\Lambda+\lambda^{2}\kappa\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{matter}}\right]\mathrm{d}x^{4},

(38)

where
\sqrt{-g}=\sqrt{-\textrm{det}g_{\alpha\beta}}
,
\lambda=1/c^{2}
, and
\bar{R}_{\mu\nu}
is the Ricci curvature tensor associated with
\boldsymbol{\bar{\nabla}}
. The matter Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{\rm matter}
is unchanged by the introduction of the reference connection.

One of the reasons Rosen introduced this theory was to be able to write a variational principle depending only on first derivatives of the physical metric (something not possible covariantly with Einstein-Hilbert action): by removing boundary terms, we can show that the second order action (
38
) is equivalent to the covariant first order action

\displaystyle S=\int\sqrt{-g}\left(2\,\mathcal{C}^{\mu}_{\alpha[\nu}\mathcal{C}^{\nu}_{\beta]\mu}g^{\alpha\beta}+\lambda\Lambda+\lambda^{2}\kappa\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{matter}}\right)\mathrm{d}x^{4},

(39)

where
\mathcal{C}^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}\coloneqq\Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}-\bar{\Gamma}^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}
, which is a tensor. Another advantage of having a second reference connection is that the usual non-covariant notion of gravitational energy used in general relativity can be made covariant
[
26
]
.

When extremalizing the action, the variation of quantities depending only on the reference connection is zero because it is a non-dynamical field. In other words, for all the paths of
\boldsymbol{g}
considered for the variation of
S
,
\bar{\Gamma}^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}
is unchanged.
For this reason, this bi-connection theory is not a bi-gravity theory
. Then, defining the energy momentum tensor as
\sqrt{-g}\,T_{\alpha\beta}\coloneqq-\delta\left(\sqrt{-g}\,\mathcal{L}_{\rm matter}\right)/\delta g^{\alpha\beta}
, we obtain the following modified Einstein equation

G_{\alpha\beta}=\lambda^{2}\kappa\,T_{\alpha\beta}-\lambda\Lambda g_{\alpha\beta}+\mathcal{T}_{\alpha\beta},

(40)

where

\mathcal{T}_{\alpha\beta}\coloneqq\bar{R}_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{\bar{R}_{\mu\nu}g^{\mu\nu}}{2}g_{\alpha\beta}

(41)

plays the role of an effective (or reference) energy-momentum tensor coming from the presence of the reference spacetime Ricci tensor. The matter energy-momentum tensor
T_{\alpha\beta}
does not depend on the reference connection, and is the usual one we consider in general relativity for perfect fluid, scalar fields, etc. Equation (
40
) also takes the simplified form

\displaystyle R_{\alpha\beta}-\bar{R}_{\alpha\beta}=\lambda^{2}\kappa\,\left(T_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{T}{2}g_{\alpha\beta}\right)+\lambda\Lambda g_{\alpha\beta}.

(42)

From the action (
38
), we also obtain the conservation of the matter energy-momentum tensor
\nabla_{\mu}T^{\mu\alpha}=0
. This is not an additional hypothesis, as it can be obtained by varying
\int\sqrt{g}\lambda^{2}\kappa\mathcal{L}_{\rm matter}\mathrm{d}^{4}x
with respect to infinitesimal changes of coordinates. The same applies for
\int\sqrt{g}\bar{R}_{\mu\nu}g^{\mu\nu}\mathrm{d}^{4}x
which leads to the conservation of the effective momentum tensor
\nabla_{\mu}\mathcal{T}^{\mu\alpha}=0
. This conservation law can be written as

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{\mu}\bar{R}_{\nu\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\alpha}\bar{R}_{\mu\nu}\right)=0,

(43)

or in the form

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}\left(\bar{\nabla}_{\mu}\bar{R}_{\nu\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\nabla}_{\alpha}\bar{R}_{\mu\nu}\right)-\bar{R}_{\alpha\mu}\mathcal{C}^{\mu}_{\sigma\gamma}g^{\sigma\gamma}=0.

(44)

The conservation of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
is an additional condition with respect to classical general relativity, called
the bi-connection condition
. In the case
\bar{R}_{\alpha\beta}=0
, it becomes trivial and we retrieve general relativity. The main interpretation for that additional equation is to constrain the diffeomorphism freedom present when defining
\bar{\Gamma}^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}
with respect to
\Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}
(see Appendix
F
for a detailed explanation).

We see that the introduction of the reference spacetime curvature tensor in the action leads to an additional term
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
in Einstein’s equation which depends only on second order (or less) derivatives of the physical metric and on an additional reference field which is the reference Ricci tensor
\bar{R}_{\alpha\beta}
. Thus we fulfill Constraint 3. presented in Section
5.1
. There remains to make a choice for
\bar{\Gamma}^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}
.

Remark 3
.

The action (
38
), respectively action (
39
), is not the most general
covariant
second order, respectively first order, action we can build from
\boldsymbol{g}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}
(contrary to the Einstein-Hilbert action for
\boldsymbol{g}
). This choice of action is driven by a physical requirement, namely we want to recover general relativity for
\bar{R}_{\alpha\beta}=0
. Thus, for instance, we could not have added
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}g^{\mu\nu}\mathcal{C}^{\beta}_{\gamma\sigma}g^{\gamma\sigma}g_{\alpha\beta}
.

Remark 4
.

We describe this theory as a bi-connection theory, and not a bi-metric theory. Indeed, the presence of a second metric is actually not essential: both the action (
39
) and the field equations (
42
) and (
43
) depend only on
\bar{\Gamma}^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}
and not on a reference metric
\bar{g}_{\mu\nu}
from which
\bar{\Gamma}^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}
would derive. In other words, in this theory, there are two ways of differentiating, but only one way of measuring distances.

5.2.2
Choice of reference connection

The choice of reference connection is the only difference between our bi-connection theory and the one of Rosen
[
25
]
. As said in the introduction, we always consider the spacetime manifold to be
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma
with
\Sigma
a closed 3-manifold. We choose
\bar{\Gamma}^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}
such that there exists a coordinate system
\{x^{0},x^{i}\}
adapted to a foliation of
\Sigma
hypersurfaces where

\bar{R}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha\nu\beta}=\delta^{\mu}_{a}\delta^{i}_{\alpha}\delta^{b}_{\nu}\delta^{j}_{\beta}\,\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{a}{}_{ibj}(x^{k}),

(45)

implying

\bar{R}_{\alpha\beta}=\delta^{i}_{\alpha}\delta^{j}_{\beta}\,\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{ij}(x^{k}),

(46)

where
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{a}{}_{ibj}
and
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{ij}
are independent of
x^{0}
. In the case
\Sigma
is a prime manifold, i.e. belonging to one of the eight Thurston classes of topology, and not a connected sum of such manifolds,
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{a}{}_{ibj}
and
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{ij}
are chosen to be the Riemann and Ricci tensors related to the natural Riemannian metric
\bar{h}_{ij}
on these topological classes. They are presented in Appendix
B
for each case. In particular, for Euclidean, spherical and hyperbolic topologies we have respectively
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathbb{E}^{3}}_{ij}=0
,
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathbb{S}^{3}}_{ij}=2\bar{h}_{ij}
and
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{ij}=-2\bar{h}_{ij}
.

Therefore, with this choice of reference connection we fulfill Constraints 1. and 2. of Section
5.1
as
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{T}}
is now directly related to the chosen spacetime topology, and that in the case of a spatial Euclidean topology, we have
\bar{R}_{\mu\nu}=0
implying
\mathcal{T}_{\mu\nu}=0
. Equation (
42
) can be interpreted as relating the deviation between the physical spacetime Ricci curvature
R_{\mu\nu}
and the reference one
\bar{R}_{\mu\nu}
to the energy content in
\mathcal{M}
. In other words, contrary to the Einstein equation for which matter directly curves spacetime, with the bi-connection theory, matter only induces a deviation of the physical spacetime curvature from the reference “topological” curvature
\bar{R}_{\mu\nu}
.
This is made more explicit in the follow up paper of this study
[
37
]
in which we will show that expansion does not depend anymore on the curvature (i.e.
\Omega=1,\ \forall\Omega_{K}
), i.e. does not depend anymore on the type of topology.

Conjecture 1
.

We conjecture that the above choice for the reference spacetime curvature corresponds to the most symmetric Riemann tensor that can be defined on a manifold with topology
\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma
. In other words, this is the Riemann tensor that admits the most independent symmetry vectors
K^{\mu}
with property

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{K}}\bar{R}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha\nu\beta}=0.

(47)

The vectors
K^{\mu}
plays the same role as Killing vectors for metric symmetries. We could call them “Riemann-Killing vectors”. If the above conjecture is true, then the choice (
45
) corresponds to the “simplest” curvature that can be defined given a specific spacetime topology. In this sense, as we do not yet have a fundamental justification for why the choice (
45
) should be made, it follows the strategy of minimal modification.

Remark 5
.

While not necessary to define the theory, it is possible to write
\bar{R}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha\nu\beta}
as deriving from a reference metric of the form
\bar{g}_{\alpha\beta}=\pm\delta^{0}_{\alpha}\delta^{0}_{\beta}+\bar{h}_{ij}(x^{k})\delta^{i}_{\alpha}\delta^{j}_{\beta}
. The signature of this metric can be either Lorentzian or Riemannian.

5.2.3
Reference vectors

For any topology, the reference spacetime Ricci tensor is of rank 3 or less. So there exists a vector
\boldsymbol{G}
such that
G^{\mu}\bar{R}_{\mu\alpha}=0
. However, the 1-form
G^{\mu}g_{\mu\alpha}
does not necessarily define a foliation in
\mathcal{M}
, i.e. it is not necessarily exact nor closed, meaning that
G^{\mu}
can have vorticity induced by the physical metric. The vector
G^{\mu}
is only uniquely defined (up to a factor) for spherical, hyperbolic,
\tilde{SL2(\mathbb{R})}
and
Nil
topologies. For the other possibilities of prime manifolds, because
\textrm{dim}[\ker(\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{ij})]>0
, then
\textrm{dim}[\ker(\bar{R}_{\alpha\beta})]>1
, and there are at least two independent vectors
\boldsymbol{G}_{(1)}
and
\boldsymbol{G}_{(2)}
such that
G_{(1)}^{\mu}\bar{R}_{\mu\alpha}=0
and
G_{(2)}^{\mu}\bar{R}_{\mu\alpha}=0
. Furthermore, any vector
\boldsymbol{G}\in\ker(\bar{R}_{\alpha\beta})
is a symmetry vector for the reference Ricci tensor:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{G}}\bar{R}_{\alpha\beta}=0.

(48)

Remark 6
.

There is also a reference exact 1-form
n_{\mu}
arising from the definition of
R^{\mu}{}_{\alpha\nu\beta}
, with property
n_{\mu}R^{\mu}{}_{\alpha\nu\beta}=0
. However, because the field equations (
42
) and (
43
) only depend on the reference Ricci tensor, the physics is blind to the presence of this 1-form.

6
Non-relativistic limit of the bi-connection theory

In this section, we show that the NR limit of the bi-connection theory is well defined for any topology (Section
6.1
), hence fulfilling Constraint 4. We also show that the NR theory obtained in the limit is the one developed in
[
36
]
(Section
6.2
), i.e. the NEN theory, and presented in Appendix
A
. Finally, we derive the dictionary between the gravitational potential in the NR limit and the metric solution of the bi-connection theory (Section
6.3
).

6.1
Equations at leading order

We consider that the reference curvature is unchanged by the limiting procedure. As explained in Appendix
F.3
, this is in general not true, but corresponds to a gauge choice, which does not change physics and the result at the limit. Then using the limit of the physical Riemann tensor and the fluid energy-momentum tensor given in sections
3.8
and
4.1
, the limit of the interchange symmetry of the physical Lorentzian structures, the modified Einstein equation (
42
) and the twice contracted second Bianchi identity give, respectively,

\displaystyle\hat{h}^{\mu\beta}{{\hat{R}}^{\alpha}}_{\gamma\mu\sigma}-\hat{h}^{\mu\alpha}{{\hat{R}}^{\beta}}_{\sigma\mu\gamma}=0,

(49)

\displaystyle\hat{R}_{\alpha\beta}-\bar{R}_{\alpha\beta}=\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\rho-\Lambda\right)\tau_{\alpha}\tau_{\beta},

(50)

\displaystyle\hat{h}^{\mu\nu}\left(\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}\hat{R}_{\nu\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\nabla}_{\alpha}\hat{R}_{\mu\nu}\right)=0.

(51)

The conservation of
T_{\mu\nu}
and of the topological term
\mathcal{T}_{\mu\nu}
, i.e. the bi-connection condition (
43
), give, respectively, at leading order

\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}\left(\rho\,u^{\mu}u^{\alpha}+\accentset{(0)}{p}\,\hat{h}^{\mu\alpha}+\accentset{(0)}{\pi}^{\,\mu\alpha}\right)=0,

(52)

\displaystyle\hat{h}^{\mu\nu}\left(\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}\bar{R}_{\nu\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\nabla}_{\alpha}\bar{R}_{\mu\nu}\right)=0.

(53)

The difference with the Newton-Cartan system is the presence of the reference spacetime curvature in equation (
50
) and the presence of the bi-connection condition (
53
). From the former, we get
\hat{R}_{\mu\beta}\hat{h}^{\mu\alpha}=\bar{R}_{\mu\beta}\hat{h}^{\mu\alpha}
, which implies that the bi-connection condition (
53
) is equivalent to equation (
51
), and, therefore, is redundant in the limit. There remains to show that these equations are well defined in non-Euclidean topologies (shown in Section
6.2.1
). For this it is sufficient to show that the spatial curvature in the limit is non-zero and compatible with the chosen topology.

As presented in Section
3.2
, the spatial curvature
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\alpha\beta}
associated to the spatial metric
\hat{h}^{\alpha\beta}
of the Galilean structure obtained in the limit is given by
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\alpha\beta}=\hat{h}^{\mu\alpha}\hat{h}^{\nu\beta}\hat{R}_{\mu\nu}
. In a coordinate system adapted to the
\tau
-foliation, its spatial components
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{ij}
correspond to the Ricci tensor associated to the spatial components
\hat{h}^{ij}
of the spatial metric. The same applies for the spatial components of
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{U}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.4811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20078pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-8.10887pt{\boldsymbol{U}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-17.96727pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.4811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20078pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{U}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.4811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20078pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-8.10887pt{\boldsymbol{U}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-17.96727pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.4811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20078pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{U}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.4811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20078pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-5.4148pt{\boldsymbol{U}}\kern 2.1029pt}}_{{\kern-12.45648pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.4811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20078pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.1029pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{U}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.4811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20078pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-4.81396pt{\boldsymbol{U}}\kern 1.50208pt}}_{{\kern-11.85565pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.4811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20078pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.50208pt}}}_{\mu\alpha}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{U}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.4811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20078pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-8.10887pt{\boldsymbol{U}}\kern where
\hat{h}_{ij}
is the inverse of

. On the other hand, in any coordinate system

with

, where we recall that

is the Ricci tensor associated to the natural metric on the covering space
\tilde{\Sigma}
(presented in Appendix

for prime manifolds).

Now, we choose
\boldsymbol{G}
such that
G^{\mu}\tau_{\mu}=1
and consider a coordinate system
\{\boldsymbol{G},\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}\}
adapted to the
\tau
-foliation
7
7

7

There always exists a vector
\boldsymbol{G}\in\ker(\bar{R}_{\alpha\beta})
such that

. The reason is that
\boldsymbol{\tau}
defines a foliation of

by
\Sigma
manifolds and that

always has a zero eigenvalue in the ‘
\mathbb{R}
-direction’.
. Then, equation (

) implies

(56)

This equation shows that the physical spatial Ricci curvature at the limit is not necessarily zero. It corresponds to a reference Ricci curvature tensor which is well defined in a non-Euclidean topology. In summary, equation (
56
) shows that
the NR limit of the bi-connection theory is well defined in non-Euclidean topologies.

6.2
Theory obtained in the non-relativistic limit

While we showed that the NR limit of the bi-connection theory is well defined for any topology, we did not show that it leads to the NR theory developed in

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\tilde{G}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Omega^{{\kern-11.37505pt{\tilde{G}}\kern 5.70836pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\tilde{G}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Omega^{{\kern-11.37505pt{\tilde{G}}\kern 5.70836pt{\alpha}{\beta}}}_{{\kern-28.04176pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.70836pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\tilde{G}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Omega^{{\kern-9.20558pt{\tilde{G}}\kern 3.5389pt{\alpha}{\beta}}}_{{\kern-25.8723pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.5389pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\tilde{G}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Omega^{{\kern-8.19446pt{\tilde{G}}\kern 2.52777pt{\alpha}{\beta}}}_{{\kern-24.86118pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.52777pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=0,

(58)

with
\hat{D}_{k}\Xi_{ij}=0
and
H(t)\coloneqq\dot{V}_{\Sigma}/(3V_{\Sigma})
with
V_{\Sigma}\coloneqq\int_{\Sigma}\sqrt{\hat{h}}\mathrm{d}x^{3}
the volume of
\Sigma
. The tensor
\Xi_{ij}
represents anisotropic expansion, and
H(t)
represents the global volume expansion.

Requiring the existence of this vector is the main improvement made in
[
36
]
compared to
[
15
]
in the development of the NEN theory. As shown in
[
36
]
, this requirement is essential for the equations of the theory to be physically coherent.

We show in the next subsections that the limit of the bi-connection theory indeed implies the existence of such a vector, called
Galilean vector
. As a side result we also correct a mistake made in
[
36
]
when constructing the NEN theory: we will show that the spatial curvature in that theory is in general homogeneous but anisotropic (even in the spherical and hyperbolic cases), while it was chosen to be necessarily isotropic in
[
36
]
.

6.2.1
Homogeneous but anisotropic spatial curvature

The equality between the reference curvature and the spatial curvature at the limit [equation (
56
)] does not imply that the spatial metric
\hat{h}_{ij}
is equivalent to the Thurston metric
\bar{h}_{ij}
. In particular, in the spherical or hyperbolic cases, equation (
56
) does not necessarily imply
\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{ij}
to be isotropic, i.e.
\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{ij}-\frac{\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{cd}\hat{h}^{cd}}{3}\hat{h}_{ij}\not=0
. As shown in Appendix
D.1
, a particular solution is

\displaystyle\hat{h}_{ij}=A\bar{h}_{ij}+A_{ij},

(59)

for
A_{ij}
a tensor satisfying
\hat{D}_{i}A=0\ ;\ \hat{D}_{k}A_{ij}=0\ ;\ A_{ij}\bar{h}^{ij}=0
. It then follows that
\hat{D}_{i}=\bar{D}_{i}
, where
\hat{D}_{i}
and
\bar{D}_{i}
are respectively the spatial connections related to
\hat{h}_{ij}
and
\bar{h}_{ij}
. In the case of spherical or hyperbolic topologies, i.e. for which
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{ij}=\pm 2\bar{h}_{ij}
, equation (
59
) leads to

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{ij}=\pm 2/A\left(\hat{h}_{ij}-A_{ij}\right),

(60)

\displaystyle\partial_{k}\left(\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{ij}\hat{h}^{ij}\right)=0.

(61)

So in general, the physical spatial curvature tensor in the NR limit of the bi-connection theory is
homogeneous but anisotropic
. It is isotropic only if
A_{ij}=0
. That anisotropy necessarily implies the presence of an anisotropic expansion (Appendix
D.2
). That property corrects a mistake made in
[
36
]
, when constructing the NEN theory, where anisotropic expansion was allowed with isotropic curvature.

We did not manage to prove that (
59
) is the general solution of (
56
). However, it is a natural choice to take, as it encompasses
\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{ij}=\hat{\mathcal{R}}\,\hat{h}_{ij}/3
for the spherical and hyperbolic topologies. In that case, equation (
50
) corresponds to the NEN equation (
74
).

Finally, from property (
48
) and equation (
55
), we have

\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{G}}\left(\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mu\nu}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{G}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.10356pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-7.97276pt{\boldsymbol{G}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-17.53172pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.10356pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{G}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.10356pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-7.97276pt{\boldsymbol{G}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-17.53172pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.10356pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{G}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.10356pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-5.31758pt{\boldsymbol{G}}\kern 2.1029pt}}_{{\kern-12.1454pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.10356pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.1029pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{G}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.10356pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-4.71675pt{\boldsymbol{G}}\kern 1.50208pt}}_{{\kern-11.54457pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.10356pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.50208pt}}}_{\alpha\mu}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{G}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.10356pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-7.97276pt{\boldsymbol{G}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-17.53172pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.10356pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{G}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.10356pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-7.97276pt{\boldsymbol{G}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-17.53172pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.10356pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{G}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.10356pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-5.31758pt{\boldsymbol{G}}\kern 2.1029pt}}_{{\kern-12.1454pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.10356pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.1029pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{G}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.10356pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-4.71675pt{\boldsymbol{G}}\kern 1.50208pt}}_{{\kern-11.54457pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.20712pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.10356pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.50208pt}}}_{\beta\nu}\right)=0,

(62)

from which equation (
51
) is redundant. Then, from (
62
), in a coordinate system adapted to the
\tau
-foliation and such that
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{t}=\boldsymbol{G}
, we have

\partial_{t|_{G}}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{ij}=0.

(63)

In the isotropic case
\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{ij}=\hat{\mathcal{R}}(t)\,\hat{h}_{ij}/3
(only possible for spherical or hyperbolic topologies), this evolution equation for the Ricci tensor implies
\hat{\mathcal{R}}(t)\propto 1/a^{2}(t)
.

6.2.2
Existence of a Galilean observer

As explained in Appendix
A
, the presence of vector with properties (
57
) and (
58
) implies (for isotropic expansion, i.e.
\Xi_{ij}=0
) the existence of a coordinate system in which
\hat{h}_{ij}(t,x^{k})
can be separated in space and time dependence
[
36
]
:
\hat{h}_{ij}(t,x^{k})=a(t)^{2}\tilde{h}_{ij}(x^{k})
. In other words, this implies the existence of inertial frames as classically defined in Newton’s theory. However, the existence of a Galilean vector is not implied by the definition of Galilean structures alone, but must come from a physical equation. Einstein’s equation implies this existence
[
34
]
, there remains to show that this is also the case from the bi-connection equations.

This is shown in Appendix
D
: any
\tau
-timelike vector
\boldsymbol{G}\in\ker(\bar{R}_{\mu\nu})
is a Galilean vector:
\boldsymbol{G}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{G}}
. That vector is only unique if
\textrm{dim}[\ker(\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{ij})]=0
(equivalently if
{\textrm{dim}[\ker(\bar{R}_{\mu\nu})]=1}
). Otherwise,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{G}}
is determined up to a spatial vector
\boldsymbol{w}
, i.e.
w^{\mu}\tau_{\mu}=0
, such that
w^{i}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{ij}=0
and
\hat{D}_{k}w^{i}=0
. In other words, in the direction of zero spatial curvature, inertial frames are defined up to a global translation, as is the case in Newton’s theory.

This concludes the proof that the NR limit of the bi-connection theory is well defined for any topology, and that the resulting NR equations correspond (in the spherical or hyperbolic cases) to a corrected version of the NEN theory presented in Appendix
A
. It is corrected in the sense that anisotropic expansion necessarily requires anisotropic spatial curvature, something not originally present in the NEN theory developed in
[
36
]
.

6.3
Dictionary

As a complementary result, in this section, we give the dictionary between the (non-Euclidean)-Newtonian theory and the spacetime metric
\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\mu\nu}
given by the NR limit of either Einstein’s equation or the bi-connection theory. The derivation is shown in Appendices
E
and
F
. We only consider Euclidean, spherical or hyperbolic topologies with isotropic expansion (i.e.
\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{ij}=2K/a^{2}\,\hat{h}_{ij}
with
K=0
or
\pm 1
, and
\Xi_{ij}=0
). We obtain that, as function of the gravitational potential
\Phi
(solution of the Poisson equation
80
), the spacetime metric components take the form

\displaystyle\accentset{\lambda}{g}^{\alpha\beta}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\lambda+2\lambda^{2}\left(\Phi+\frac{1}{2}\hat{D}^{k}\sigma\hat{D}_{k}\sigma+\partial_{t|_{G}}\sigma\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\lambda^{3}\right)&-\lambda\hat{D}^{i}\sigma+\mathcal{O}\left(\lambda^{2}\right)\\
-\lambda\hat{D}^{i}\sigma+\mathcal{O}\left(\lambda^{2}\right)&\begin{array}[]{c}\hat{h}^{ij}+\lambda\left[2(\Phi-H\sigma)\hat{h}^{ij}\right.\\
\left.-2\hat{D}^{i}\hat{D}^{j}E-2\hat{D}^{(i}F^{j)}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\lambda^{2}\right)\end{array}\end{array}\right)

and

\displaystyle\accentset{\lambda}{g}_{\alpha\beta}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\frac{1}{\lambda}-2\left(\Phi+\partial_{t|_{G}}\sigma\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\lambda\right)&-\hat{D}_{i}\sigma+\mathcal{O}\left(\lambda\right)\\
-\hat{D}_{i}\sigma+\mathcal{O}\left(\lambda\right)&\begin{array}[]{c}\hat{h}_{ij}-\lambda\left[2(\Phi-H\sigma)\hat{h}_{ij}\right.\\
\left.\quad-2\hat{D}_{i}\hat{D}_{j}E-2\hat{D}_{(i}F_{j)}+\hat{D}_{i}\sigma\hat{D}_{j}\sigma\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\lambda^{2}\right)\end{array}\end{array}\right),

where by definition
\hat{D}_{k}F^{k}=0
. The variables
E
,
F^{i}
and
\sigma
are gauge fields and can be set to zero without change in the physics, while we can show that
\Phi
is gauge invariant (Appendix
F
) as expected for the gravitational potential. This dictionary is valid in a coordinate system
\{\boldsymbol{\partial}_{t},\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}\}
adapted to the
\tau
-foliation and where
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{t}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{G}}
. In other words, in an inertial coordinate system (where
\hat{h}_{ij}=a(t)^{2}\bar{h}_{ij}(x^{k})
).

This is exactly the same dictionary between Newton’s theory and the spacetime metric given by Einstein’s equation. In particular, in the gauge choice
\sigma=0
, we retrieve the classical formula “
g_{00}=-\frac{1}{\lambda}-2\Phi
”. This holds for the gravitational potential
\Phi
calculated in either a Euclidean topology (with Newton’s theory), or in a spherical/hyperbolic topology (with the NEN theory). Furthermore, this also shows that the gravitational slip (i.e. the difference between the potential in
g_{00}
and in the factor in front of
\hat{h}_{ij}
) is zero (see Appendix
F.2
for more details). This is an important result as this parameter is usually used as a discriminator between different gravity theories. Therefore, once again, this shows that Newton’s theory and the NEN theory are algebraically equivalent, as the equations defining the theories (see the spatial NEN equations in Appendix
A.2
) and their dictionaries are equivalent, with the only (implicit) difference being the curvature of the spatial metric and the topology on which they are defined.

Remark 7
.

This dictionary is only a non-relativistic dictionary, and not a ‘post-non-relativistic’ dictionary (we could also use the term ‘post-(non-Euclidean)-Newtonian’). Post-non-relativistic terms are present at higher orders, in the space components of
\accentset{(2)}{g}^{\,\mu\nu}
and the time component of
\accentset{(1)}{g}_{\mu\nu}
, which are not considered here.

7
Summary of results and conclusion

The results and logic followed during this paper are summarised in the evolution of the scheme presented in Figure
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
, and
5
.

The first aim of the paper was to study the existence of a NR limit of the Einstein equation as function of the topology (Question (2) of introduction). We showed that such a limit only exists if the spatial topology is Euclidean, in the sense of the Thurston decomposition, i.e. its universal cover is
\mathbb{E}^{3}
. We argued that this result can be interpreted as a signature of an inconsistency of general relativity for non-Euclidean topologies (Section
4.3
), in other words Einstein’s equation would be physically wrong if the spatial topology of the Universe would be non-Euclidean.

The second goal was to search for a relativistic equation for which the limit exists in any topology (Question (3) of introduction). We proposed four constraints that such an equation should fulfill (Section
5.1
) and gave a solution. While this might not be the only answer to question (3) fulfilling the constraints given, we think it is the simplest solution, essentially because no new dynamical field in added to general relativity. The theory is a bi-connection theory defined by the equations

\displaystyle R_{\alpha\beta}-\bar{R}_{\alpha\beta}=\lambda^{2}\kappa\,\left(T_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{T}{2}g_{\alpha\beta}\right)+\lambda\Lambda g_{\alpha\beta},

(70)

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}\left(\nabla_{\mu}\bar{R}_{\nu\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\alpha}\bar{R}_{\mu\nu}\right)=0.

(71)

The Ricci curvature
\bar{R}_{\alpha\beta}
is related to a reference non-dynamical connection
\bar{\Gamma}^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}
chosen as a function of the spacetime topology (which is assumed to be of the form
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma
with
\Sigma
closed): its Riemann tensor takes the form

\displaystyle\bar{R}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha\nu\beta}=\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{a}{}_{ibj}(x^{k})\delta^{\mu}_{a}\delta^{i}_{\alpha}\delta^{b}_{\nu}\delta^{j}_{\beta},

(72)

in a coordinate system adapted to a foliation of
\Sigma
-hypersurfaces, where
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{a}{}_{ibj}
is the Riemannian curvature relative to the covering space
\tilde{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
. In this sense,
\bar{R}_{\alpha\beta}
is a
topological term
added to the Einstein equation.

In conclusion, the above bi-connection theory is compatible with the non-relativistic regime in any topology. Without other candidates and because of its simplicity, this relativistic theory should be used if one wants to study a model universe with a non-Euclidean spatial topology and requiring the NR limit to exist. As for now, the only reason for considering this theory is related to the hypothesis of full compatibility between the relativistic and the NR regimes. It would be interesting to have a more fundamental origin for the presence of the reference spacetime curvature. We expect such a justification to come either from a purely mathematical approach as proposed in Section
4.3.2
with a “topological Lagrange multiplier”; or from physics with quantum gravity theories.

In the follow up paper of this study
[
37
]
, we analyse the consequences for cosmology of the bi-connection theory, in other words the consequences for cosmology of requiring full compatibility between the relativistic and the non-relativistic regimes. In particular, we show a major result: the expansion becomes blind to the spatial curvature (i.e.
{\Omega=1,\ \forall\Omega_{K}}
), hence giving an elegant solution to the flatness problem.

Appendix A
The NEN theory

In this section, we present the properties of the non-relativistic theory in non-Euclidean topologies, i.e. the NEN theory, developed in
[
15
]
and
[
36
, section 5.6]
.

A.1
Spacetime equations

The theory is defined on a 4-manifold
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}\times\Sigma
where
\Sigma
is closed.
\mathcal{M}
is equipped with a Galilean structure
(\hat{h}^{\alpha\beta},\tau_{\mu},\hat{\nabla}_{\nu})
as defined in Section
3.2
. The spacetime equations of the NEN theory are

\displaystyle\hat{h}^{\mu\beta}{{\hat{R}}^{\alpha}}_{\gamma\mu\sigma}-\hat{h}^{\mu\alpha}{{\hat{R}}^{\beta}}_{\sigma\mu\gamma}=0,

(73)

\displaystyle\hat{R}_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}{3}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-9.18335pt{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-25.85007pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-9.18335pt{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-25.85007pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-7.76959pt{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}\kern 2.1029pt}}_{{\kern-24.43631pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.1029pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-7.16876pt{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}\kern 1.50208pt}}_{{\kern-23.83548pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.50208pt}}}_{\alpha\beta}=\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\rho-\Lambda\right)\tau_{\alpha}\tau_{\beta},

(74)

\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}T^{\mu\alpha}=0,

(75)

where
\rho
is the matter density;
T^{\alpha\beta}
is the energy-momentum tensor;
\Lambda
is the cosmological constant;
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-9.18335pt{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-25.85007pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-9.18335pt{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-25.85007pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-7.76959pt{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}\kern 2.1029pt}}_{{\kern-24.43631pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.1029pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-7.16876pt{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}\kern 1.50208pt}}_{{\kern-23.83548pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.50208pt}}}_{\alpha\beta}
is the orthogonal projector (defined by relation (
6
)) to a vector
\tilde{G}^{\alpha}
with
{\tilde{G}^{\mu}\tau_{\mu}\coloneqq 1}
called
the Galilean vector
(or Galilean observer) in
\mathcal{M}
. It is defined by two conditions: (i) in coordinates adapted to the
\tau
-foliation and such that
\boldsymbol{\partial}_{t}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{G}}
, the time derivative of the spatial components of the space metric (which correspond to the expansion tensor of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{G}}
) are

\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\hat{h}^{ij}=-H(t)\hat{h}^{ij}-\Xi^{ij},

(76)

and (ii)
\tilde{\boldsymbol{G}}
is vorticity free with respect to the Galilean structure, i.e.

\hat{h}^{\mu[\alpha}\hat{\nabla}_{\mu}\tilde{G}^{\beta]}=0.

(77)

H(t)
is the expansion rate of the finite volume of the spatial sections defined by
\boldsymbol{\tau}
, and
\Xi^{ij}
is traceless-transverse and represents anisotropic expansion. Property (
76
) ensures that, without anisotropic expansion, there exists an adapted coordinate system (the one related to
\tilde{{\boldsymbol{G}}}
) in the NEN theory such that the spatial metric can be separated in space and time dependence:
h_{ij}(t,x^{k})=a(t)^{2}\tilde{h}_{ij}(x^{k})
. This coordinate system defines what we call an
inertial frame
in classical Newton’s theory. Therefore, property (
76
) ensures its existence for the non-Euclidean version of the theory
8
8

8

In
Vigneron 2022b
, we give a fully covariant definition of the Galilean vector, which is equivalent to property (
76
).
.

Equation (
73
) is the Trautmann condition, also present in Newton’s theory with the Newton-Cartan system. It ensures that no gravitomagnetism is present in the theory. In equation (
74
),
\frac{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}{3}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-9.18335pt{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-25.85007pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-9.18335pt{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}\kern 3.51666pt}}_{{\kern-25.85007pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.51666pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-7.76959pt{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}\kern 2.1029pt}}_{{\kern-24.43631pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.1029pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}b^{{\kern-7.16876pt{\boldsymbol{\tilde{G}}}\kern 1.50208pt}}_{{\kern-23.83548pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.50208pt}}}_{\alpha\beta}
is an additional term with respect to the Newton-Cartan system. This term was added by
[
15
,
36
]
for the theory to be defined on spherical or hyperbolic topologies, as it implies the spatial Ricci tensor (from equation (
74
)) to have the form
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{ij}=\pm 2/a(t)^{2}\,\hat{h}^{ij}
, where
a
is the scale factor of expansion.

A.2
Spatial equations

To allow for a more intuitive understanding of this theory, we present the spatial equations resulting from the above the spacetime equations. Assuming isotropic expansion (
\Xi_{ij}=0
), and introducing the gravitational potential
\Phi
defined via the 4-acceleration of the Galilean vector (i.e.
\hat{D}^{\mu}\Phi\coloneqq\tilde{G}^{\nu}\hat{\nabla}_{\nu}\tilde{G}^{\mu}
), we have (see
[
36
]
for a detailed derivation)

\displaystyle\dot{v}^{a}

\displaystyle=-\hat{D}^{a}\Phi-2v^{a}H+(a_{\not=\rm grav})^{a},

(78)

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}/\rho

\displaystyle=-3H-\hat{D}_{c}v^{c},

(79)

\displaystyle\hat{\Delta}\Phi

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{2}\left(\rho-\left\langle\rho\right\rangle_{\Sigma}\right),

(80)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{ab}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pm 2}{a(t)^{2}}\hat{h}_{ab},

(81)

with the expansion law

\displaystyle 3\ddot{a}/a

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa}{2}\left\langle\rho\right\rangle_{\Sigma}+\Lambda,

(82)

where the dot derivative corresponds to
\partial_{t}+v^{c}\hat{D}_{c}
with
\hat{D}_{i}
the covariant derivative with respect to the spatial metric;
\boldsymbol{v}
is the spatial fluid velocity;
(a_{\not=\rm grav})^{a}
is the non-gravitational spatial acceleration of the fluid;
\left\langle\psi\right\rangle_{\Sigma}(t)\coloneqq\frac{1}{V_{\Sigma}}\int_{\Sigma}\psi\sqrt{\mathrm{det}(\hat{h}_{ij})}\mathrm{d}^{3}x
is the average of a scalar field
\psi
over the volume
V_{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
. This system is valid in an inertial frame, i.e. in coordinates where the spatial metric is separated is space and time dependence.

We see that the spatial equations defining the NEN theory are algebraically equivalent to the cosmological Newton equations, but with the presence of a non-zero spatial Ricci tensor related to a spherical or hyperbolic topology. This shows that the NEN theory can be seen as an adaptation of Newton’s theory (by adding a curvature) such that it is defined on non-Euclidean topologies.

Appendix B
Natural metrics on Thurston’s topological class

We give the natural metrics for each Thurston’s topological class and some properties of their Ricci tensor (we denote
N_{\ker{}}\coloneqq\dim[\ker(\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{ij})]
):

•

For Euclidean topologies (the covering space
\tilde{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
is
\mathbb{E}^{3}
):

\bar{h}_{ij}=\delta_{ij},

(83)

with
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{ij}=0
and
N_{\ker{}}=3
.

•

For
\tilde{\Sigma}=\mathbb{S}^{3}
(spherical topologies):

\bar{h}_{ij}\mathrm{d}x^{i}\mathrm{d}x^{j}=\mathrm{d}r^{2}+\sin^{2}r\left(\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}\right),

(84)

with
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{ij}=2\bar{h}_{ij}
and
N_{\ker{}}=0
.

•

For
\tilde{\Sigma}=\mathbb{H}^{3}
(hyperbolic topologies):

\bar{h}_{ij}\mathrm{d}x^{i}\mathrm{d}x^{j}=\mathrm{d}r^{2}+\sinh^{2}r\left(\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}\right),

(85)

with
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{ij}=-2\bar{h}_{ij}
and
N_{\ker{}}=0
.

•

For
\tilde{\Sigma}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
:

\bar{h}_{ij}\mathrm{d}x^{i}\mathrm{d}x^{j}=\mathrm{d}r^{2}+\sin^{2}r\,\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}+\mathrm{d}z^{2},

(86)

with
\bar{\mathcal{R}}=2
,
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{\langle ij\rangle}\not=0
and
N_{\ker{}}=1
.

•

For
\tilde{\Sigma}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{H}^{2}
:

\bar{h}_{ij}\mathrm{d}x^{i}\mathrm{d}x^{j}=\mathrm{d}r^{2}+\sinh^{2}r\,\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}+\mathrm{d}z^{2},

(87)

with
\bar{\mathcal{R}}=-2
,
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{\langle ij\rangle}\not=0
and
N_{\ker{}}=1
.

•

For
\tilde{\Sigma}=\tilde{SL2\mathbb{R}}
:

\bar{h}_{ij}\mathrm{d}x^{i}\mathrm{d}x^{j}=\mathrm{d}x^{2}+\cosh^{2}x\,\mathrm{d}y^{2}+\left(\mathrm{d}z+\sinh^{2}x\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{2},

(88)

with
\bar{\mathcal{R}}=-5/2
,
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{\langle ij\rangle}\not=0
and
N_{\ker{}}=0
.

•

For
Nil
topologies:

\bar{h}_{ij}\mathrm{d}x^{i}\mathrm{d}x^{j}=\mathrm{d}x^{2}+\mathrm{d}y^{2}+\left(\mathrm{d}z-x\mathrm{d}y\right)^{2},

(89)

with
\bar{\mathcal{R}}=-1/2
,
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{\langle ij\rangle}\not=0
and
N_{\ker{}}=0
.

•

For
Sol
topologies:

\bar{h}_{ij}\mathrm{d}x^{i}\mathrm{d}x^{j}=e^{-2z}\mathrm{d}x^{2}+e^{2z}\mathrm{d}y^{2}+\mathrm{d}z^{2},

(90)

with
\bar{\mathcal{R}}=-2
,
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{\langle ij\rangle}\not=0
and
N_{\ker{}}=2
.

In this section, the notion of spatial is related to the foliation defined by
\boldsymbol{\tau}
and to the metric
\hat{\boldsymbol{h}}
present in the Galilean structure.

C.2
Expansion and vorticity tensors

The formalism of this section is presented in more details in
[
34
]
.

For a Galilean structure, the expansion
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{U}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.4811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20078pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Theta^{{\kern-10.73112pt{\boldsymbol{U}}\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-20.58952pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.4811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20078pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.13892pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{U}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.4811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20078pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Theta^{{\kern-10.73112pt{\boldsymbol{U}}\kern 6.13892pt}}_{{\kern-20.58952pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.4811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20078pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.13892pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{U}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.4811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20078pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Theta^{{\kern-7.123pt{\boldsymbol{U}}\kern 3.81111pt}}_{{\kern-14.16469pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.4811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20078pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.81111pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\boldsymbol{U}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.4811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20078pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Theta^{{\kern-6.03412pt{\boldsymbol{U}}\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-13.0758pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.40157pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.4811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.20078pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.72223pt}}}^{\alpha\beta}
and vorticity
